95. Overarm throw-and-catch

Target & send

When: May, June · Slot: 16–25 · 1 ball, 1 hoop catcher.

Students throw overarm to a catcher in a hoop, then swap roles. Use it to practise the overarm throw and ready hands.

Equipment: Playground ball, hoop.

Set-up: Throw line and a hoop catcher. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Throwing line behind a cone. Fetchers wait to the side — never in the throwing lane.
  2. Throw to the catcher in the hoop. Catcher runs the ball home. Roles swap.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Overarm throw-and-catch in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Call ‘mine.’
  • Opposite-foot step.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Variations

  • Underhand for 1–2.
  • Step back one giant step after each make (grades 3–6).
  • Opposite hand or weaker foot for the last round.
  • Team score is ‘makes in two minutes’ — not a ranking of throwers.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Overarm throw-and-catch.
  • Watch for fetchers walking through the throwing line. Make them a job to the side.
  • Year plan: May, June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Throw to the hoop. Catcher runs it home. Swap.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Overarm throw-and-catch for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Catcher is ready; thrower steps.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Sends toward the target, not a face. Walks to collect. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls the catch.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

96. Poison Ball

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Grades 3–6 · 6 min · 6–10 dodgeballs.

Throwers outside the circle aim below the waist, and a hit dodger switches places with the thrower. Use it to practise throwing and dodging with nobody out for the game.

Equipment: Dodgeballs, a cone circle.

Set-up: 6–8 dodgers inside. Others throw from outside the circle. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Throws stay below the waist.
  2. A hit = switch places with that thrower.
  3. A catch = the thrower comes in and the catcher throws.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Below the waist or a two-hand catch is safe.
  • A hit walks you to the other team — you stay in the game.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Variations

  • 4-corner dodge.
  • Must bounce the ball first.
  • Step back one giant step after each make (grades 3–6).
  • Opposite hand or weaker foot for the last round.
  • Team score is ‘makes in two minutes’ — not a ranking of throwers.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Poison Ball.
  • Watch for fetchers walking through the throwing line. Make them a job to the side.
  • Year plan: January, March — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Four teams or two. Dodgeballs.
  • A below-waist hit sends you to the team that threw it (or to a sideline stretch, then back in).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Poison Ball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Dodgeballs.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Hits stay low; a hit is a stretch, then back in.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Do not play with throws at people. Use Battleships.
Grades 3–4. Below the waist. Continuous switch.
Grades 5–6. Timed 2-minute waves. Count how long a group stays in.

Safety. No last-one-standing as the only version. Dodgeballs. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.

Grades 3–4. Own cone. Time cap.

99. Prison Ball

Target & send

When: December, January · Slot: 16–25

A hit sends you to the prison behind the other team. Hit someone from prison and you come home.

Equipment: Foam balls, cones for the prison lines

Set-up: Two teams, one half each. Cone a prison zone behind each end line.

How we play

  1. If you are hit below the waist, go to the prison behind the other team.
  2. From prison you may throw at the team in front of you. A hit brings you home and sends that player to prison.
  3. Balls come from the front and from behind — check all directions.
  4. Play timed games.
  5. House rule: below-the-waist hits with foam balls. A hit is a 10-second stretch or a trip to jail with a clear way back — nobody sits for long. Full rules sit on the Dodgeball page.

Video. Prison Ball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 4-8

Equipment: Foam Balls

A dodgeball-type game for two teams. Each team has a prison area at the far end of the gym, behind the other team. A player who is hit goes to the prison behind the other team. They stay there until they throw a ball from prison and hit someone; then they return home, and the player they hit goes to prison. Players must watch in front, behind and to the sides because balls come from all directions.

  1. Two teams, each half. Behind each end is a PRISON zone.
  2. If you are hit with a foam ball (below shoulders), go to the prison BEHIND the other team.
  3. From prison you may throw at the team in front of you. If you hit someone, you return home and they go to prison.
  4. Balls fly from front and behind, so players must check all directions.
  5. Play timed games. Foam balls only.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Not for grades 1–2 — use Poison Ball (roll only) or a target game instead.
Grades 3–4. Rolls or underhand throws below the knees. Short 2–3 minute rounds with a full reset.
Grades 5–6. Play as written with house rules. Students can referee a line or count hits.

Safety. Foam balls only. Throws below the waist; no head shots and no close-range throws. A hit is a short stretch or a jail with a clear way back — nobody sits the period. Fetch balls along the side. Dodgeball is optional programming: use it only where it fits the group.

100. Protect the Castle

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Pins or cones as the castle · soft balls · 2–4 defenders · attackers outside

Attackers knock the castle. Defenders block. Rotate before anyone lives in one job.

Also called: Protect the House · Guard the Cone · Protect the King

Equipment: Pins or cones · soft balls · 2–4 defender bibs

Set-up: Castle in the middle (pin, cone, or ‘king’ in a hoop). Defenders around it. Attackers behind a line with balls.

How we play

  1. On go, attackers throw or roll to knock the castle.
  2. Defenders block with hands or body. They may not hold the target on.
  3. Knockdown = 1. Reset.
  4. Rotate defenders every 60–90 seconds.

If this happens

  • Throw at a face: that thrower sits one reset.
  • Defender holds the pin on: no block, reset.
  • Nobody can score: must roll, or add a second castle.

How a round ends. Most knockdowns, or longest defender shut-out.

Cues

  • Defenders catch or block — no body-check.
  • Attackers throw below the shoulders.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Noodle balanced on the cone (Daly house).
  • King in a hoop; two-pass before a shot.
  • Below-waist only.
  • Soccer or hockey ball on sport days.
  • Guard the Cone: one cone, same rotate.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Protect the House; Guard the Cone; Protect the King.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Protect the Castle (Prime Coaching Sport)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Protect the Castle for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Underhand or below-shoulder send to a target.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Fetchers are not in the throwing lane.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Below-waist throws at pins, not people.
Grades 5–6. Two balls at once, still below waist.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

101. Punt to the line

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Sport ball, one lane.

Students drop-kick a football toward a line 15–20 m away and keep kicking until it crosses. Use it to practise the punt.

Equipment: Football or playground ball, a line.

Set-up: 15–20 m to a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One shooter at a time. Next waits behind the home cone. Fetchers stay to the side of the pin.
  2. Drop and kick before the bounce. Chase. Kick again until it crosses. Run it home.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Punt to the line in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Drop, then kick.
  • Chase your own ball.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Variations

  • Place-kick for 1–2.
  • Step back one giant step after each make (grades 3–6).
  • Opposite hand or weaker foot for the last round.
  • Team score is ‘makes in two minutes’ — not a ranking of throwers.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Punt to the line.
  • Watch for fetchers walking through the throwing line. Make them a job to the side.
  • Year plan: January, March — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Kick toward the line. Kick again if needed. Run it home.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Punt to the line for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Drops and kicks before a bounce, or places it.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild kick.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Kicks away from other lanes.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

102. Rolling 21

Target & send

When: December, January · Slot: 16-25

Bowling with a bust rule. Large hoop 3, small hoop 7. Exactly 21.

Equipment: Pairs: large hoop, small hoop, one ball

Set-up: Two hoops between the pair.

How we play

  1. Take turns. Large = 3, small = 7, miss = 0.
  2. Add the points aloud.
  3. Past 21 = return to 15.
Grades 1–2. Play to 15. Only the large hoop.
Grades 3–4. Exactly 21. Bust to 15.
Grades 5–6. Team of three sharing one total.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle.

103. Skittle Ball

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Standing pins or cones · balls · a fetch line

Knock a pin, then either count it or collect it.

Also called: Skittles · Knock the Pin · Build Your Tower · Pin Knockover · Hockey pin knockdown · Roll to hit the pin

Equipment: Standing pins or cones · balls · a fetch line

Set-up: Pins out. Throwers on a mark. One fetcher at a time.

How we play

  1. Underhand throw or roll at a pin.
  2. Knockdown = 1 point, or collect that pin and stack it at your base.
  3. Fetcher resets or delivers. Next thrower.
  4. Continue until pins are gone or time ends.

If this happens

  • Two pins from one ball: take both only if you have said so.
  • Human wall in front of every pin: send them back to the mark.
  • Tower collapses: rebuild from the fallen pins, no extra collect.

How a round ends. Most pins, tallest tower, or points in a time cap.

Cues

  • One throw, then fetch.
  • Safe at the pin line.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Run a small triangle before the throw (Daly Knock the Pin).
  • Kick the ball (Pin Galore).
  • Build a tower from collected cones.
  • Hockey pin knockdown on hockey days.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Skittles; Knock the Pin; Build Your Tower; Pin Knockover; Pin Galore Soccer; Hockey pin knockdown; Roll to hit the pin.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Wild Ball Pin Knockover (PhysEdGames) · Pin Guard / Defender (PE Game Changers) · Pin Knockover (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details: Pin Knockover · Grades 2-8

Equipment: Plastic bowling pins, dodgeballs

A target throwing or rolling game for two teams, one on each side of the gym. Each team tries to be first to knock over all of the other team’s pins. Skills include blocking, goaltending, running, and underhand and overhand throwing. Set up the same number of pins on both sides. Mark a line in front of the pins so students cannot guard right behind it. Add the dodgeballs. The first team to knock over all of the other team’s pins wins. A pin a player knocks over on their own side stays down. Play many rounds.

  1. Two teams, each with a row of plastic pins on its end. Foam dodgeballs.
  2. Throw or roll to knock down the other team’s pins. First team to knock all opponent pins wins, or most pins down at time.
  3. Players may block and “goal tend” in front of their own pins. No crossing mid-line if you want a throwing-only game.
  4. Reset pins and play again. Skills: underhand, overhand, blocking, ducking.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Skittle Ball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Underhand or below-shoulder send to a target.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Fetchers are not in the throwing lane.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

Source

104. Slide Sling Save

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Two teams · 6 min · beanbags.

Students slide beanbags at the other team’s feet, and a hit player returns by catching a ball thrown by a teammate. Use it to practise sliding, throwing and catching.

Equipment: Beanbags, dodgeballs, two jail hoops.

Set-up: Two halves. Slide from behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Slide beanbags at the other team’s feet.
  2. A hit player walks to the far jail hoop.
  3. A teammate throws a dodgeball for them to catch — a catch brings them back.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Slide at feet.
  • A hit walks to jail; a teammate catch frees you.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Variations

  • Must bounce once.
  • Step back one giant step after each make (grades 3–6).
  • Opposite hand or weaker foot for the last round.
  • Team score is ‘makes in two minutes’ — not a ranking of throwers.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Slide Sling Save.
  • Watch for fetchers walking through the throwing line. Make them a job to the side.
  • Year plan: January, March — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Slide beanbags along the floor at feet. A hit player walks to a jail hoop.
  • A teammate may catch a bag to free one jailed player.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Slide Sling Save for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Dodgeballs stay below the waist; a catch is a save.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Hits stay low; a hit is a stretch, then back in.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Slides on the floor only. Hits at feet, not shins with force. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

The classic two-team version, played here with house rules so nobody sits the period.

106. Survivor Dodgeball

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Grades 3–6 · short rounds.

A below-the-head hit means the player switches teams or does a 10-count stretch and stays in. A caught throw puts the thrower out. Use it to practise throwing, catching and dodging.

Equipment: Many dodgeballs.

Set-up: Two sides or a circle. Same house rules as Poison Ball. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Throwing line behind a cone. Fetchers wait to the side — never in the throwing lane.
  2. Hit below the head and that player switches teams or does a 10-count stretch and stays.
  3. A catch is a save. The thrower is out.
  4. Start: on GO, take a ball and carry it back to your wall before you throw.
  5. If all the balls end up on one side, the game ends; the side without the balls wins.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  7. Play Survivor Dodgeball in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Same house rules as Poison Ball.
  • Re-enter after a stretch.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Variations

  • Doctor: one teammate may unfreeze with a two-hand touch.
  • Catch = the thrower is out.
  • Step back one giant step after each make (grades 3–6).
  • Opposite hand or weaker foot for the last round.
  • Team score is ‘makes in two minutes’ — not a ranking of throwers.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Survivor Dodgeball.
  • Watch for fetchers walking through the throwing line. Make them a job to the side.
  • Year plan: January, March — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • A hit is 10 jumping jacks, then back in, or join the thrower’s team.
  • Keep 4–6 balls in play. Freeze on the whistle.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Survivor Dodgeball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Dodgeballs.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Hits stay low; a hit is a stretch, then back in.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Do not play. Use Clean Your Room with rolls.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Timed sides. No last-one-standing.

Safety. Dodgeballs. Below the head. No head shots. A hit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

107. Team Bowling

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Hoops or pins with values · balls or bags · a throw line

Land in the target. Add the points. Rotate.

Also called: Rolling 21 · Hoop bowling · Circuit Lights · Land the ball in the hoop · Chip into buckets

Equipment: Hoops or pins with values · balls or bags · a throw line

Set-up: Targets out. Agree values (any hoop = 1, or big = 3 and small = 7). Throw line marked.

How we play

  1. Take turns sending the ball.
  2. Call the running total aloud.
  3. Miss = 0. Next player.

If this happens

  • Ball on the rim: teacher call.
  • Forgot the score: rewind to last agreed total.

How a round ends. First team to the target score, or most in a time cap.

Cues

  • Everyone throws.
  • Count, reset, switch sides.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Exactly 21. Over 21 drops you to 15 (Rolling 21).
  • Slide the bag into cones to ‘turn lights on.’
  • Kick instead of throw.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Rolling 21; Hoop bowling; Circuit Lights; Land the ball in the hoop; Chip into buckets.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Team Bowling for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Underhand or below-shoulder send to a target.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Fetchers are not in the throwing lane.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

109. Tidy the Bedroom

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · Two halves · equal light objects · centre line · optional hoops

Your half is tidy when the whistle goes.

Also called: Tidy Up · Messy Rooms · Mr. Clean · Mr Clean

Equipment: Equal light objects · centre line · optional hoops

Set-up: Centre line. Equal objects. Stay on your side.

How we play

  1. On go, send objects onto the other side or into tidy hoops.
  2. Gentle throws. No crossing. No throwing at people.
  3. Whistle after 60–90 seconds. Count what is left on your half.

If this happens

  • Object at a person: that thrower sits the next bout.
  • Step over the line: that object comes back.
  • Two objects in two hands if you have said one: extra goes back.

How a round ends. Fewer objects on your side wins.

Cues

  • Underhand into the drawer.
  • One piece of clothing at a time.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Chase a moving bucket and put balls in one at a time (Daly Tidy Up).
  • One team tidies, one scatters.
  • Clothespins clipped onto backs (Mr. Clean).

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Tidy Up; Messy Rooms; Mr. Clean; Mr Clean.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Clean Your Room (PhysEdUniverse)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Tidy the Bedroom for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Sends stay below the waist.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild throw.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Hands up on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Step back after each fair throw.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. No overhand smash. Below the waist if scattering. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. 110. Tunnel kick knockdown

Target & send

When: January, March · Slot: 16–25 · 3–5 even teams or pairs · 8–12 minutes · fetchers stay to the side of the throwing line.

Students dribble through a tunnel, take one controlled kick at a pin, then dribble home. Use it to practise dribbling and accurate kicking.

Equipment: Cone tunnel, one pin or stacked cone, soccer ball.

Set-up: Tunnel to a wall or pin line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One shooter at a time. Next waits behind the home cone. Fetchers stay to the side of the pin.
  2. Dribble the tunnel. One kick at the pin. Dribble home. Team keeps going until the pin is down.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Tunnel kick knockdown in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • One kick.
  • Fetch from the side.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.

Variations

  • Kick from a line for 1–2.
  • Step back one giant step after each make (grades 3–6).
  • Opposite hand or weaker foot for the last round.
  • Team score is ‘makes in two minutes’ — not a ranking of throwers.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Tunnel kick knockdown.
  • Watch for fetchers walking through the throwing line. Make them a job to the side.
  • Year plan: January, March — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Tunnel, one shot, dribble home. Keep going until the pin falls.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Tunnel kick knockdown for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Plant foot beside the ball.
  • Skill focus. Chooses a target and a force — close and soft, or far and firm — not a wild kick.
  • Skill focus. Waits a turn; collects on the call so the next player can send.
  • Character Development. Records or resets honestly; a miss is another try.
  • Safety. Fetchers stay out of the lane.
  • Healthy Relationships. Cheers a classmate’s send; does not block someone from a turn.
Grades 1–2. Shorter tunnel. Kick from a line.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

113. Barkball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Two teams · 6–8 min · sport ball.

Players hit or kick, then run to the far line and back while fielders tag with the ball held in two hands. Use it to practise striking, running and fielding.

Equipment: Playground or t-ball, bat or kick, an end line.

Set-up: Hitting team and fielding team. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Hit or kick, then run to the far line and back if you can.
  2. Fielders may not throw at the runner. They tag with the ball in two hands.
  3. Rotate after everyone has hit.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Call before you catch.
  • Below-shoulder sends.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Must use the other team’s names (harder listen).
  • Quiet mouths for one round — hand signals only.
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Barkball.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • A noisy sending game: you must call a teammate’s name before they can catch.
  • A silent catch does not count. Turn over on a drop.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Barkball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. 3-second hold, then pass.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to run, hold, or send to a base or hoop.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact. Bat or kicker away from the waiting line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls a name before the catch.
Grades 1–2. Kick only. Run to first line only.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Fielders call ‘mine.’ Rotate the strike job every three batters. Add a force-out at one base.

Safety. No slide tackles, no body checks. The ball is not thrown at a face. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

114. Castleball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Two halves · structures (pins, cone-castles, benches) · soft balls

Stay on your half. Knock the other team’s structures. Rebuild and keep playing.

Also called: Bombardment · Star Wars · Snowball Fight · Castleball 2.0

Equipment: Equal castles each side · soft balls

Set-up: Equal castles each side. Balls on each half. Below-the-head or structure-only throws.

How we play

  1. On go, throw at the other castles.
  2. 1 point per castle down. That team rebuilds while play continues.
  3. Whistle. Count standing castles or points.

If this happens

  • Throw at a person: no point, warning.
  • Cross the centre: that throw does not count.
  • All down in 10 seconds: play two bouts and add.

How a round ends. Most standing castles, or most knockdowns.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Opposite-foot step. Eyes on the target, not the teacher.
  • Wait for the fetch before the next throw.
  • One object in the air at a time per pair or lane.
  • Below the head. A high ball is dead.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Beach ball only, push it onto the other bench (Star Wars).
  • Cone targets on a centre bench; touch your wall before you throw.
  • Retrievers vs throwers with a target hoop (Snowball Fight).
  • Bombardment / Battleball: same halves, same rebuild.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Bombardment; Battleball; Star Wars; Snowball Fight; Castleball 2.0.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

Video. Bombardment (PhysEdGames) · Castleball (PhysEdGames) · Castleball 2.0 (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 4-8

Equipment: Dodgeballs, hoops

A target game with a strong teamwork and strategy component. It also uses throwing, catching, blocking, building and awareness. Make two teams, one on each half of the gym; players never leave their own side. First, have students practise building castles from hoops: each castle uses 6 hoops (1 base, 4 sides, 1 top). It takes patience and teamwork to build a sturdy one. Each team then builds 2 castles at the back of its area. Add the dodgeballs. On the signal, players throw at the other team’s castles. Score a point each time a castle falls. Players rebuild a fallen castle while the game continues. Play as long as you like and add changes as needed.

  1. Two teams, each must stay on its own half.
  2. Practice building a 6-hoop castle: 1 hoop base, 4 standing side hoops, 1 roof hoop.
  3. Each team builds 2 castles toward the back of its area, then add foam dodgeballs.
  4. On the signal, throw at the other team’s castles to topple them. 1 point per castle down.
  5. When a castle falls, that team rebuilds while play continues. Play a set time.

Source · Video

More details: Castleball 2.0 · Grades 3-8

Equipment: balls, hoops, cones

A variation of Castleball with set roles. Set up the castles, choose roles, throw the “cannon balls” and rebuild together, so there is a job for everyone. Idea credit: Deric Hafer.

  1. Each team builds hoop castles in its back zone (6 hoops: 1 base, 4 walls, 1 roof).
  2. Assign roles: builders, throwers/cannons, gatherers, guards.
  3. On GO, cannons throw foam balls to topple the other castle. Builders only rebuild — they do not throw.
  4. Rotate roles every 2–3 minutes.
  5. 1 point each time a castle is fully toppled. Rebuild and continue.

Source · Video

More details: Bombardment · Grades 3-7

Equipment: variety

A multi-skill game that mostly uses basketball skills and can be adapted. Idea credit: Dan Kirsch.

  1. Two teams, each defending a half and a row of targets (pins, cones, or stacked items) near their end line.
  2. Scatter basketballs or foam balls at mid-court.
  3. On the whistle, players dribble/pass (basketball skills) and throw or shoot to knock down the other team’s targets.
  4. Score 1 point per target down, or capture the fallen target and add it to your side (pick one scoring rule).
  5. No player-to-player contact. Defenders may block balls and gather rebounds.
  6. Reset targets after a time cap or when one side is cleared. Modify to soccer-style (feet only) if you want.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Castleball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Throws low at a structure; ready hands to block or catch.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to throw and when to rebuild.
  • Skill focus. Takes a turn throwing and rebuilding; rotates jobs without fuss.
  • Character Development. Honest count of castles down; no arguing a call.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact; hits stay below the head.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a loose ball; helps a teammate rebuild a castle.
Grades 1–2. Short throwing line. Underhand or roll is allowed. Teacher fetches with the first group.
Grades 3–4. Standard distance. Opposite-foot step. Fetchers stay to the side.
Grades 5–6. Step back after a make. Weaker hand or weaker foot on the last round. A student official on the line.

Safety. Throwing line is closed until fetchers are to the side. One object in the air per lane. Foam dodgeballs. Hits below the head. A high ball is dead. A hit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

115. Catch & Step

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · 1-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

Partners step back after each catch and step forward after a drop. Use it to practise throwing and catching as the challenge grows.

Equipment: Type of ball, or water balloons

Set-up: Partners start close and play catch with a ball (or, outdoors in warm weather, a water balloon). Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. After every successful catch, both players take one step backward.
  2. On a drop, they take a step forward (or, if you are using beanbags, that pair is wet and rejoins with a ball).
  3. See which pair ends farthest apart after a time cap.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.
  • Open-hand tap. Do not catch or punch the balloon.

Variations

  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Catch & Step.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Catch & Step (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 1-8

Equipment: Type of ball, or water balloons

A throwing and catching game that gets harder as players succeed. Partners face each other about 30 cm (1 foot) apart with a ball, or a water balloon outdoors. They toss back and forth. After a catch, the catcher steps back; after a miss, the player who missed steps forward. See how far apart pairs can get. Play with a ball first, and try water balloons outside once students are skilled.

  1. Partners start close and play catch with a ball (or, outdoors in warm weather, a water balloon).
  2. After every successful catch, both players take one step backward.
  3. On a drop, they take a step forward (or, if you are using water balloons, that pair is wet and rejoins with a ball).
  4. See which pair ends farthest apart after a time cap.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Catch & Step for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Sends a catchable throw; ready hands on a catch.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to step back and when to step forward.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns throwing and catching with a partner.
  • Character Development. Honest about a catch or a drop; no arguing a step.
  • Safety. Throws stay away from faces; balloons stay in the pair’s square.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ before the catch; encourages a partner after a drop.
Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Fielders call ‘mine.’ Rotate the strike job every three batters. Add a force-out at one base.

Safety. The ball is not thrown at a face. Balloons stay in the pair’s square. Tie extra balloons so they are not mouthed. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

Source

116. Chuck the Chicken

117. Continuous Kick Ball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Two teams · 10 min · playground ball, 3–4 hoops as bases.

Several runners can be on base at once, and play stops when the kicker’s partner places the ball back on the cone. Use it as a kickball game that keeps more runners moving.

Equipment: Playground or t-ball, saucer cone, bases or hoops.

Set-up: Two teams. Each kicker has a partner on the fielding team. Ball starts on a cone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Kick the ball off the cone and run. Several runners may be on base at once.
  2. Anyone may field, but only the kicker’s partner may place the ball back on the cone to stop play.
  3. Runners caught between bases when the ball is replaced are out of that run — they rotate, they do not sit the inning.
  4. A catch in the air is an out for that kicker only; other runners keep going until the cone is reset.
  5. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Everyone kicks.
  • Throw to the base, not the runner.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • One-base and back (Quick Baseball).
  • Tee instead of a roll.
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Continuous Kick Ball.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Pitcher rolls. Kicker kicks and runs. There are no strikeouts — a miss is a re-roll or a walk to first after two misses.
  • Fielders get an ‘out’ by catching a fly, or by getting the ball to the base the runner is going to.

Video. Kickball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Continuous Kick Ball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Everyone gets a kick or hit.
  • Skill focus. Ball goes to a base or hoop, not at the runner.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact. Bat or kicker away from the waiting line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a runner who is unsure of the next hoop.
Grades 1–2. Everyone runs to first only. Teacher helps reset the cone.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Full diamond. Force at two bases.

Safety. No slide tackles, no body checks. The ball is not thrown at a face. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

118. Diamond Strike

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Grades 3–6 · 10 min · four hitters start on bases.

A fielder bowls underhand from the middle, and all four hitters run one base on any hit. Use it to practise striking and fielding with continuous play.

Equipment: 4 bases or buckets, t-ball, bats or hands.

Set-up: A square. One hitter at each base. Fielders around. A middle cone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. A fielder bowls underhand from the middle to any hitter.
  2. On a hit, all four hitters run one base clockwise.
  3. A catch in the air, or a ball placed on a base before a runner arrives, rotates that hitter to the waiting line.
  4. Everyone hits before teams swap.
  5. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • One hit, everyone moves one base.
  • Field to the middle cone.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Must hit to the opposite field.
  • Force at every base.
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Diamond Strike.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Four hitters occupy the bases. One hit (or kick) and all four run one base.
  • Fielders return the ball to a middle cone to stop the next bowl.

Video. Diamond Cricket (The Games Gurus)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Diamond Strike for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Everyone gets a kick or hit.
  • Skill focus. Ball goes to a base or hoop, not at the runner.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact. Bat or kicker away from the waiting line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a runner who is unsure of the next base.
Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Fielders call ‘mine.’ Rotate the strike job every three batters. Add a force-out at one base.

Safety. Bowl from the middle only. No throw at runners. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

119. Floorball

Strike & field

When: November · Slot: 8-15

Score in the opponent's mini goal and defend your own.

Equipment: Floor hockey sticks, soft ball, mini goals

Set-up: Goals at each end. One ball.

How we play

  1. Tap the ball with the stick.
  2. After a goal, the conceding player restarts.
  3. Freeze on the whistle.
Grades 1–2. Hands instead of sticks.
Grades 3–4. Wider goals.
Grades 5–6. 3v3 with sidelines.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle.

120. Hit and Run

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Stations or a small diamond · 8 min.

Players hit or kick, then run to a hoop and back while fielders throw to the hoop, not at the runner. Use it to practise striking, running and fielding.

Equipment: Tee or kick, t-ball, one or two hoops.

Set-up: Hitting line. Fielders spread. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Hit or kick. Run to a hoop and back.
  2. Fielders throw to the hoop, not at the runner.
  3. Everyone hits.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Contact, then look up.
  • Field to a hoop.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Opposite-side hits.
  • Must slide-stop at the hoop (no dive).
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Hit and Run.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Hit or kick, run a short marked path.
  • Fielders throw to a hoop, never at the runner.

  • Active Living. Stays in Hit and Run for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.

  • Skill focus. Ball goes to a base or hoop, not at the runner.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact. Bat or kicker away from the waiting line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a runner who is unsure of the next hoop.
Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Fielders call ‘mine.’ Rotate the strike job every three batters. Add a force-out at one base.

121. Jailbreakers

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Two teams · 6–8 min.

A runner who is stopped walks to jail, and the next fair hit frees one jailed runner. Use it to practise striking and fielding.

Equipment: Tee or kick, t-ball, a jail hoop.

Set-up: Hitting team and fielding team. Jail hoop beside first. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Hit or kick and run.
  2. If the field stops the ball fairly, the runner walks to jail.
  3. The next fair hit frees one jailed runner.
  4. Everyone bats, then swap.
  5. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Rescue is a tag and a walk home.
  • One rescue at a time.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Two-hand join on the walk.
  • Jailbreak: one special player may free the whole jail once.
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Jailbreakers.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Tagged players go to a jail. A teammate tags the first person in line and walks them home.
  • You cannot steal an object while you are on a rescue walk.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Jailbreakers for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Strikes or kicks to a space; ready hands or feet on a catch or stop.
  • Skill focus. One object or one rescue at a time.
  • Skill focus. Walks a jailed teammate home.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact. Bat or kicker away from the waiting line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a runner who is unsure of the next hoop.
Grades 1–2. Kick off a cone. Jail is one hoop.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Fielders call ‘mine.’ Rotate the strike job every three batters. Add a force-out at one base.

Safety. Throw to a hoop, not at the runner. Bat in a hoop. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

122. Kick It (mats)

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Two teams · 8 min · mats as bases.

Players kick, then run from mat to mat while fielders tag with the ball held in two hands. Use it to practise kicking, running and fielding.

Equipment: Two mats, playground ball.

Set-up: Mats 10–15 m apart. Kickers in a line. One roller. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Roll, kick, run to the far mat.
  2. Fielders tag with the ball held in two hands — they do not throw at the runner.
  3. A fair run scores. Next kicker. Swap after everyone kicks.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Tag with the ball in two hands.
  • No throw-at-runner.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Must bounce the ball once before a tag.
  • Mats closer for Grade 3.
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Kick It (mats).
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Kick, then run mat to mat.
  • A fielder tags a runner by touching them with the ball held in two hands.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Kick It (mats) for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Everyone gets a kick or hit.
  • Skill focus. Ball goes to a base or hoop, not at the runner.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact. Bat or kicker away from the waiting line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a runner who is unsure of the next mat.
Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Runners may try a return run on the next kick.

Safety. No slide tackles, no body checks. The ball is not thrown at a face. Walk on and off mats. No diving onto a house mat. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

123. Longball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · 4-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

Play this kickball-style game with foam balls, or a foam ball and plastic bat, and extra running rules so more players move more often. Use it to practise kicking, running and fielding.

Equipment: 2 mats, indoor soccer balls or Softex foam balls

Set-up: Two mats as far bases / safe zones. Indoor soccer balls or Softex foam balls (or a foam ball + plastic bat). Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Kicker puts the ball in play and runs. Extra movement rules keep more people running than classic kickball (watch the video for the exact running pattern they use).
  2. Fielders play the ball to stop the run. Use a tag-with-ball or throw-to-mat out, not a hard hit.
  3. Kick the next ball quickly so the field never waits. Switch sides after a time cap rather than 3 outs if you want more activity.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.
  • Small touches. Foot stays near the ball.

Variations

  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Longball.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Longball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 4-8

Equipment: 2 mats, indoor soccer balls or Softex foam balls

A kickball-style game (or a baseball-style game with an oversized plastic bat and a foam ball). Its changes to regular kickball keep more players moving more often. Some strategy matters, but the focus is on everyone taking part. Idea credit: Sabine Crandall.

  1. Two mats as far bases / safe zones. Indoor soccer balls or Softex foam balls (or a foam ball + plastic bat).
  2. Kicker puts the ball in play and runs. Extra movement rules keep more people running than classic kickball (watch the video for the exact running pattern they use).
  3. Fielders play the ball to stop the run. Use a tag-with-ball or throw-to-mat out, not a hard hit.
  4. Kick the next ball quickly so the field never waits. Switch sides after a time cap rather than 3 outs if you want more activity.

Source · Video

  • Movement Skill Development. Strikes or kicks to a space; ready hands on a catch.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to run and when to hold.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact; tags with the ball or throws to the mat, not a hard hit.

Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.

124. Massive Kickball Dodgeball

Strike & field

When: June, December · Slot: 16–25

Kickball meets dodgeball for up to about 50 players. Kick, run to a mat, and dodge the fielders' throws.

Equipment: 2 foam balls, exercise mats as bases

Set-up: Two teams. Exercise mats as bases or islands around the gym. Two foam balls.

How we play

  1. A kicker kicks one ball and runs to a mat.
  2. Fielders may throw a foam ball to hit the runner below the waist. A runner on a mat is safe.
  3. A hit runner jogs back to the kicking line and joins the end of the kicking queue — no sitting out.
  4. Keep both balls live. Switch kicking and fielding after a set number of kickers.

Video. Massive Kickball Dodgeball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 4-8

Equipment: 2 foam balls, exercise mats

A large-group game (up to 50 players) that combines kickball and dodgeball. Use it when you have a very large group and want something different.

  1. Two teams. Use exercise mats as bases/islands. Two foam balls.
  2. A kicker kicks one ball and runs to a mat. Fielders may throw a foam ball to hit the runner below the shoulders.
  3. A hit runner is out. A runner who reaches a mat is safe.
  4. Keep both balls live so the field never stalls. Switch kicking/fielding after a set number of kickers.
  5. Works with up to about 50 players.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Not for grades 1–2 — use Poison Ball (roll only) or a target game instead.
Grades 3–4. Rolls or underhand throws below the knees. Short 2–3 minute rounds with a full reset.
Grades 5–6. Play as written with house rules. Students can referee a line or count hits.

Safety. Foam balls only. Throws below the waist; no head shots and no close-range throws. A hit is a short stretch or a jail with a clear way back — nobody sits the period. Fetch balls along the side. Dodgeball is optional programming: use it only where it fits the group.

Source

126. Shark Ball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · 4-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

A minnow kicks the foam ball and runs for the far line while the sharks field the ball and try to tag. Use it as a kickball game mixed with Sharks and Minnows (idea from Kevin Williams).

Equipment: 1 foam ball

Set-up: Mark two safe end lines with a large open “ocean” in the middle. One foam ball only. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Pick 2–4 sharks as fielders / taggers. Everyone else is a minnow / kicker.
  2. A minnow kicks the foam ball and immediately runs toward the far safe line.
  3. Sharks field the ball. With the ball they may tag a runner (ball-in-hand tag or a below-shoulder throw — decide first).
  4. A tagged runner becomes a shark. A runner who reaches the far line is safe and rejoins the kicking line.
  5. Keep the next kicker ready so the game never stops.
  6. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  7. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Shark Ball.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Shark Ball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 4-8

Equipment: 1 foam ball

A kickball variation mixed with Sharks and Minnows. Idea credit: Kevin Williams.

  1. Mark two safe end lines with a large open “ocean” in the middle. One foam ball only.
  2. Pick 2–4 sharks as fielders / taggers. Everyone else is a minnow / kicker.
  3. A minnow kicks the foam ball and immediately runs toward the far safe line.
  4. Sharks field the ball. With the ball they may tag a runner (ball-in-hand tag or a below-shoulder throw — decide first).
  5. A tagged runner becomes a shark. A runner who reaches the far line is safe and rejoins the kicking line.
  6. Keep the next kicker ready so the game never stops.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Shark Ball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Strikes or kicks to a space; ready hands on a catch.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to run and when to hold.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest out or safe; no arguing a force.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact; throws at a runner stay low.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a runner who is unsure of the next hoop.
Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Fielders call ‘mine.’ Rotate the strike job every three batters. Add a force-out at one base.

Safety. No slide tackles, no body checks. The ball is not thrown at a face. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

127. Strikeball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · K-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

In a circle, players strike or roll the ball to send it between another player’s legs while that player tries to stop it. Use it to practise striking, goaltending and reaction time.

Equipment: Ball

Set-up: Players stand in a circle, feet wide, one ball in play. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. The player with the ball strikes / rolls it, trying to send it between another player’s legs.
  2. That player tries to stop it with hands or by closing the gap (agree the legal save before you start).
  3. A ball that goes through scores a point for the striker, or the defender does a quick exercise — pick one.
  4. Keep the ball moving. Works in several circles for a large class.
  5. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Strikeball.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Strikeball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-8

Equipment: Ball

A circle game for goaltending, fielding, striking and reaction time. Groups of 4–8 stand in a circle, each player’s feet touching the feet of the players beside them. Give each group a ball. Keeping the ball inside the circle, players strike it with a hand and try to send it between another player’s legs. Players may stop the ball with their hands or arms but may not close their legs. The player scored on gets the ball. Play for a set time or score.

  1. Players stand in a circle, feet wide, one ball in play.
  2. The player with the ball strikes / rolls it, trying to send it between another player’s legs.
  3. That player tries to stop it with hands or by closing the gap (agree the legal save before you start).
  4. A ball that goes through scores a point for the striker, or the defender does a quick exercise — pick one.
  5. Keep the ball moving. Works in several circles for a large class.

Source · Video

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Strikeball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Strikes or rolls to a gap; ready hands on a stop.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to strike and when to close the gap.
  • Skill focus. Takes a turn with the ball; passes it on so everyone strikes.
  • Character Development. Honest about a score or a stop; no arguing a save.
  • Safety. Looks up after contact; the ball stays away from faces.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls a name before sending; helps a classmate who is unsure of the legal save.
Grades 1–2. Tee or a bounced toss. Run to one base or hoop and stay. Teacher fields the first few.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.

Safety. The ball is not thrown at a face. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.
