128. Thunderball

Strike & field

When: June · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · 6 min · wiffle or t-ball, 30 cones.

Players hit or kick the ball through three rows of cones for 1, 2 or 3 points while the defence stops it. Use it to practise striking and fielding.

Equipment: Wiffle or t-ball, bat or kick, 30 cones in 3 rows.

Set-up: Three rows of cones across the gym. Hitting team on one end. Defence in the lanes. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Each hitter tries to send the ball through the rows.
  2. Past row 1 = 1 point, row 2 = 2, row 3 = 3.
  3. Defence stops the ball before the next row. Everyone hits, then swap.
  4. Balls come back along the side. Restart on a teacher call, not a scramble through the middle.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Hit through the rows, not at people.
  • Defence stops the ball before it crosses.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Ready position in the field — feet still, hands out.
  • Hit, then run the path. Do not watch your own hit.
  • Fielders send to the base or hoop, not at a runner.

Variations

  • One hitter at a time; next batter ready.
  • Tee or a bounced teacher-toss for grades 1–2.
  • Three-pitch maximum, then a run-out anyway so the line keeps moving.
  • Fielders rotate one spot after each batter.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Thunderball.
  • Watch for a long batting line. Use a continuous format or a 3-pitch max.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Three cone rows. Hit or kick. Past row 1 = 1 point, row 2 = 2, row 3 = 3.
  • Defence in the lanes stops the ball before the next row.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Thunderball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Sends the ball through a row, not at a person.
  • Skill focus. Knows when to send through a gap in the rows.
  • Skill focus. Takes batting order and a fielding spot; rotates so the same student is not always pitcher.
  • Character Development. Honest row count; no arguing a call.
  • Safety. Hitters wait behind the line; no full bats indoors.
  • Healthy Relationships. Calls ‘mine’ on a fly; helps a defender cover the next row.
Grades 1–2. Kick off a cone. One row only.
Grades 3–4. Live send (kick, strike, or throw). Three chances, then a run so the line moves.
Grades 5–6. Live toss. Defence must pass once before they can stop it.

Safety. Hitters wait behind a line. No full hard bats indoors — wiffle or t-ball only. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

129. Circle run relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Even teams of 4–6 · 8–12 m lanes · two full rotations then a quality round (not a ranking).

One runner laps the circle, then high-fives the next runner. Use it for sustained running with fair hand-offs.

Equipment: Cone circle.

Set-up: Large loop, start cone. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. One runner laps the circle. High-five. Next goes.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Circle run relay in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Pass outside.
  • High-five.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Walk-jog for 1–2.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Circle run relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • One lap. Next runner.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Keeps a pace they can finish.
  • Movement Skill Development. Repeats the named locomotor or send with control to the line and back.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the high-five, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Tags the next runner, not a shortcut.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; the leg continues to the end.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a slow leg.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Choose a pace they can keep.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

130. Cone Relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Teams of 4 · 5 min · a stack of cones.

Call an order such as 2-4-3-1, and the runner touches the cones in that order and returns. Use it to practise memory and speed.

Equipment: Numbered cones 1–4.

Set-up: Cones in a square or line. Relay teams. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Teacher calls an order such as 2-4-3-1.
  2. The runner touches that order and returns to tag.
  3. New order each turn.
  4. High-five the next runner at home. First team sitting quietly after two rotations earns a cheer, then play a quality round.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • One cone per trip.
  • Build a tidy stack.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Must sidestep.
  • Upside-down stacks only.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Cone Relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Carry one cone to the far line, set it, come back, tag.
  • Next round: unstack and bring them home.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Cone Relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Waits behind the line for the tag.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the tag, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Hands off — does not throw at a teammate.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; the leg continues to the end.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a slow leg.
Grades 1–2. Two cones. Teacher points as well as calls.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Students write the next order.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

131. Crab-bear walk relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Short lanes · 3 rounds.

Students crab walk to the far cone and bear walk home in short lanes. Use it to practise two animal walks in a short bout.

Equipment: Two cones per lane.

Set-up: 8–10 m lanes. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. Crab walk (belly up) to the far cone. Bear walk (hips up) home. High-five.
  3. Wrists get a stretch after two rounds.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. Play Crab-bear walk relay in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Belly up on the crab.
  • Hips up on the bear.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Knees down if wrists complain.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Crab-bear walk relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Crab out, bear back, stretch wrists.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Crab-bear walk relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Hips stay off the floor on the crab.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the high-five, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line; high-fives the next runner kindly.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; the leg continues to the end.
  • Safety. Stops when wrists complain.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a slow leg.
Grades 1–2. Very short. Knees down if wrists complain.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Switch which animal goes first.

Safety. Hips stay off the floor on the crab. Stop if wrists hurt. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

132. Dash and Dribble

Relays & stations

When: September, January · Slot: 16–25 · Relay teams of 4 · 6 min.

Students dribble to the cone, trap the ball, then sprint back to tag the next player. Use it to practise dribbling and trapping.

Equipment: Soccer balls, cones.

Set-up: Relay lines. A cone 8–12 m out. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. Dribble to the cone, trap, sprint back, tag.
  3. Next player goes.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. Play Dash and Dribble in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Trap at the cone before you turn.
  • Hand off — don’t roll at a teammate.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Weak foot only.
  • Add a hoop step-in.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Dash and Dribble.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: September, January — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Dribble to a cone, trap, sprint back, tag the next hand.
  • If the ball runs away, recover it and keep the turn — no sitting out.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Dash and Dribble for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Ball stays close; trap or dribble under control.
  • Skill focus. One ball at a time; looks before sending.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line; tags the next runner kindly.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Eyes up at the turn. No long lines — more teams. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

134. Fetch Relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25 · File of runners · item pile · home hoop or sack

One item per trip. Tag. Next person.

Also called: Attention Relay · Bucket Carry · Around the Bases Relay · Cone Flipper · Three Course Meal · Cone Collector · Post the Parcel

Equipment: Item pile · home hoop or sack

Set-up: Pile at the far end. Home hoop at the start. Show what ‘one item’ looks like.

How we play

  1. On go (and on a colour if you are using colours), first runner fetches one legal item, places it home, tags.
  2. Wrong item goes back.
  3. Continue until the pile is gone or time ends.

If this happens

  • Two items: extra goes back.
  • Sack or bucket left behind: return for it.

How a round ends. Most correct items, or first team finished.

Cues

  • Wait for the call.
  • Start on your number or colour.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Teacher calls the colour after go.
  • Bib-sack must travel.
  • Roll the ball around a slalom cone (Snowball).
  • Flip three cones, three tries each (Cone Flipper).
  • Move one ‘course’ one hoop forward (Three Course Meal).
  • Cone balanced on one foot home (Cone Collector).
  • Around the Bases: one item is a touch of each base.
  • Bucket Carry: the bucket must travel with the item.
  • Post the Parcel: hide five beanbags under a cone 5–10 m away; each runner carries a tennis ball out, leaves it under the cone, brings a beanbag back and tags the next runner; done when every beanbag is swapped.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Attention Relay; Bucket Carry; Around the Bases Relay; Cone Flipper; Three Course Meal; Cone Collector.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Attention Relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Waits behind the line for the tag.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the tag, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Hands off — does not throw at a teammate.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Add a skill on the loop (3 jumps, a skip).

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

135. Hockey hurdle dribble

Relays & stations

When: September, November, January · Slot: 16–25 · Low cones, sport ball.

Students push the ball under or through a wavy line of low cones with the blade on the floor. Use it to practise small hockey touches.

Equipment: Low cones as ‘hurdles,’ stick, hockey ball.

Set-up: Wavy line of low cones. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. Push the ball under or through the cones. Blade down. No slapshot.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Hockey hurdle dribble in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Blade down.
  • Tiny touches.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Feet-only for 1–2.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).
  • Feet-only hockey-ball version if sticks are new or the class is tired.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Hockey hurdle dribble.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Sticks: teach grip and ‘blade down’ in the hallway line before the first dribble.
  • Year plan: September, November, January — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Push through the cones. No slapshot.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Hockey hurdle dribble for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Small touches through the gates.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the high-five, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line; high-fives the next runner kindly.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Blade stays on the floor.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Feet-only if sticks are new.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Two-touch through each gate.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. Sticks stay below the knee. No slapshots. Blade on the floor when you stop. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

136. Hoop Relay

Relays & stations

When: February · Slot: 16–25 · Lane of hoops · optional ball, stick, or shared hoop

Advance hoop to hoop with the agreed action. Next runner waits for the signal.

Also called: Toe-tap hoop relay · Circle Hoop Pass · Circle Hoop · Hop-the-hoops relay · Rabbit Relay · Hoop Stick · Caterpillar Relay

Equipment: Lane of hoops · optional ball, stick, or shared hoop

Set-up: Hoops in a lane. Show the action once (jump in, pass then jump, stick-push, shared carry, step through).

How we play

  1. Complete the action in hoop 1, then hoop 2, and so on.
  2. Drop or miss = redo that hoop.
  3. Sit or high-five at the end to release the next runner.

If this happens

  • Early start: back to hoop 1.
  • Hands on a no-hands carry: restart that pair.

How a round ends. First team with everyone through.

Cues

  • Taps in the hoop.
  • Switch feet.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Bag must stay in the hoop before you jump in.
  • Hoop pinched between two stomachs, drop it over a cone.
  • Growing caterpillar steps through after each out-and-back.
  • Circle Hoop Pass: pass the hoop down a line without breaking hands.
  • Hop-the-hoops: two-foot jumps only.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Toe-tap hoop relay; Circle Hoop Pass; Circle Hoop; Hop-the-hoops relay; Rabbit Relay; Hoop Stick; Caterpillar Relay.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Toe-tap hoop relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Sole stays on the ball for the taps.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the high-five, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line; high-fives the next runner kindly.
  • Character Development. Finishes the taps before running.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

137. Hoop underhand relay

Relays & stations

When: February · Slot: 16–25 · Beanbags and two hoops.

Each runner gets three underhand tries to land a beanbag in the target hoop. Use it to practise underhand accuracy.

Equipment: Beanbags, two hoops.

Set-up: Home hoop and a target hoop. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. Take a beanbag. Three underhand tries into the target. Leave a make. Home.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Hoop underhand relay in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Step to the target.
  • Underhand.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Opposite hand for 5–6.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Hoop underhand relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Three tries. Leave a make.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Hoop underhand relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Steps toward the hoop.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the high-five, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line; high-fives the next runner kindly.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Waits behind the line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

138. Hop-the-hoops relay

Relays & stations

When: September, February, May · Slot: —

One-foot hop with a planned landing.

Equipment: 6 hoops per team.

Set-up: Hoops almost touching in a line.

How we play

  1. Hop on the right foot to the end. Hop on the left foot home.
Grades 1–2. Step if a hop is too much.
Grades 3–4. Hop both ways.
Grades 5–6. Two-foot jump out, hop back.

Safety. Quiet land. No pushing a hoop into the next jumper.

139. Hungry Snake

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · One snake of 4–6 · 5 min.

Each team stays linked in a line while the head collects objects and passes them back to the tail. Use it to practise teamwork and moving together.

Equipment: 20–40 beanbags or yarn balls, 2 bags or bins.

Set-up: Two teams in two lines, each student holding the shirt or shoulders of the person in front. Objects scattered. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. On go, the snake slithers. Only the head may pick up an object.
  2. Pass it back person to person into the tail bag.
  3. If the snake breaks, freeze, reconnect, then go.
  4. High-five the next runner at home. First team sitting quietly after two rotations earns a cheer, then play a quality round.
  5. Play Hungry Snake in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Hold waists or a hoop train — no yanking.
  • The head tags.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Must stay on lines.
  • Two snakes from the start.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Hungry Snake.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • A small chain is the snake. The head tags runners. A tagged runner joins the tail.
  • Split at 6 into two snakes.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Hungry Snake for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. The chain splits before it gets unsafe.
  • Skill focus. Starts on go, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Holds on without yanking.
  • Character Development. Only the head picks up a drop; the snake reconnects and continues.
  • Safety. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the snake; does not shame a break or a drop.
Grades 1–2. Slow waves. Short snakes of 4.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Travel the whole gym. No talking round.

Safety. No running while linked in a long chain. Break at 6 if it whips. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

140. Ice Cream Cone Relays

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · K-4 · 5–8 min rounds.

Run three ice-cream relays: scoop stack, build the cone and take the order. Use them for team building, a single lesson or a field-day rotation.

Equipment: foam balls, cones

Set-up: Split the class into even relay teams. Give each team a home marker and foam balls (scoops) plus cones (the waffle cones). Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Relay A — Scoop stack: Runner 1 carries one foam ball to the far cone, balances it as a scoop, and sprints back to tag the next player. First team to stack the set number of scoops without a collapse wins.
  2. Relay B — Build the cone: Cones start nested at home. Runners take turns carrying one cone out and stacking a taller ice-cream cone at the far end. Rebuild if it falls.
  3. Relay C — Take the order: Teacher calls an order (“3 scoops”). The runner must carry that many balls in one trip, place them on the cone, and return.
  4. Change locomotor skills between relays (run, skip, gallop). Use as a field-day rotation or a single lesson.
  5. High-five the next runner at home. First team sitting quietly after two rotations earns a cheer, then play a quality round.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Ice Cream Cone Relays.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.

141. Jump to Hoop Race

Relays & stations

When: February, May · Slot: 16-25 · Daly Exercise · slot 16-25.

Jump hoop-to-hoop; next runner waits for a sit at the end. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Jump to Hoop Race

Equipment: One hoop per player as stepping-stones

Set-up: Hoops in a lane. Team behind hoop 1.

How we play

  1. Jump to the finish and sit.
  2. Sitting starts the next teammate.
  3. First team all seated wins.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Step-in allowed.
  • Larger hoops.
  • Travel back as well.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Jump to Hoop Race for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Jump to Hoop Race with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Jump to Hoop Race instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Jump to Hoop Race.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Jump to Hoop Race.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Step-in allowed.
Grades 3–4. Larger hoops.
Grades 5–6. Travel back as well.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

142. Jungle Relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Even teams · a turn cone · optional bag or cone for the back

Each runner uses a real animal movement. Disguised sprinting does not count.

Also called: Jungle Run Relays · Animal Crawl · Turtle Race · Rabbit Hole

Equipment: Turn cone 8–15 m away · optional bag or cone for the back

Set-up: Show 6 legal animals. Separate lanes. Cone 8–15 m away.

How we play

  1. First player animal-moves to the cone and back, tags.
  2. Next player must pick a different animal unless you have allowed repeats.
  3. Whole team goes.

If this happens

  • Disguised sprint: send them back to do the animal.
  • Bag or cone falls off the back: replace and continue from there.

How a round ends. First team home, or fewest drops.

Cues

  • Complete the course.
  • Hand-off, not a shout.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Cone on the mid-back (Turtle).
  • Crawl under a hoop-on-cones (Rabbit Hole).
  • Class as tunnels and bridges, other half travels.
  • Animals: pick from a posted list.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Jungle Run Relays; Animals; Animal Crawl; Turtle Race; Rabbit Hole.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

  • Active Living. Stays in Jungle Run Relays for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Waits behind the line for the tag.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the tag, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Hands off — does not throw at a teammate.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. 143. Jungle Run Relays

Relays & stations

When: May · Slot: 16–25

Relay with a hoop, a jump, and a cone.

144. Pip, Squeak and Wilbur

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class in a circle or three teams · 5 min.

Call one trio name, and that player runs around the circle, through an arch, to the centre beanbag. Use it for short, frequent relay turns.

Equipment: Beanbags or letter cards in the centre.

Set-up: Trios in a big circle. Name the three players Pip, Squeak, and Wilbur. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Call one name. That player runs the circle, through an arch made by the other two, and to the centre beanbag.
  2. Reset and call another name.
  3. Short, frequent turns.
  4. High-five the next runner at home. First team sitting quietly after two rotations earns a cheer, then play a quality round.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Listen for your name.
  • The three names chase in order.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Whole-class locomotor when your name is called.
  • Add a fourth name.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Pip, Squeak and Wilbur.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Give every student one of three names. Call a name to move; the next name in the story chases.
  • Or: three students are Pip, Squeak, and Wilbur and run a chase around a circle while the others are a still wall.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Pip, Squeak and Wilbur for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the send to the object.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the call, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits for their name; holds a steady arch for a teammate.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Moves on the call, not before.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Add a letter to collect and spell a class word.

Safety. Run outside the circle. Arch is arms, not a trip-line. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

145. Rescue Relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Teams of 4–5 · 6 min.

A runner crosses, takes a waiting teammate by the hand and brings them home, and the rescued player goes next. Use it to practise running and teamwork.

Equipment: None, or a beanbag to carry.

Set-up: Relay teams. Half the team on the far line. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. First runner crosses, takes a waiting teammate by the hand, and both return.
  2. The rescued player then becomes the next rescuer.
  3. Continue until the whole team is home.
  4. High-five the next runner at home. First team sitting quietly after two rotations earns a cheer, then play a quality round.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.

Cues

  • Go get one teammate and bring them home.
  • Two-hand join or a hoop for two.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Must crab the last rescue.
  • Hoop trains only.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Rescue Relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • One runner brings the next teammate back (joined hands or sharing a hoop).
  • Then those two rescue the third, and so on.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Rescue Relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Waits on the far line for a teammate’s hand.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the hand-hold, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Hands off — does not throw at a teammate.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Silent rescue or weak-hand hold.

Safety. Hands only, no pulling a teammate off their feet. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

146. Side to Side

Relays & stations

When: January, March, June · Slot: 16-25 · Daly Exercise · slot 16-25.

Pass, then both side-jump into the next hoop. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Side to Side

Equipment: Line of hoops; 1 ball per pair

Set-up: Facing partners in hoop 1 of a ladder.

How we play

  1. Underarm or chest pass.
  2. After a catch, both jump sideways to the next hoop.
  3. Finish, sit, next pair goes.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Clap then jump, no ball.
  • Larger hoops.
  • Bounce pass only.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Side to Side for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Side to Side with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Side to Side instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Side to Side.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss, a drop, or a line in Side to Side.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle. No throws at a face.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss or a drop.
Grades 1–2. Clap then jump, no ball.
Grades 3–4. Larger hoops.
Grades 5–6. Bounce pass only.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

147. Soccer weave relay

Relays & stations

When: September · Slot: 16–25 · Even teams of 4–6 · 8–12 m lanes · two full rotations then a quality round (not a ranking).

Students weave a soccer ball out through cones with small touches, then dribble straight home. Use it to practise close ball control.

Equipment: 6 cones, one soccer ball per team.

Set-up: Zig-zag cones. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. Weave out. Straight dribble home. Foot stays near the ball.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. Play Soccer weave relay in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Small touches.
  • Head up.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Weaker foot home in 5–6.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Soccer weave relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: September — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Weave out. Straight home.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Soccer weave relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Keeps the ball in the lane.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the high-five, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line; high-fives the next runner kindly.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. No slide tackles.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Balls stay on the floor. No slide tackles. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

148. Speed Run Relay

Relays & stations

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25 · Many teams of 3–4 · 5 min.

Students sprint to a cone, do 3 jumps or a touch, then sprint back to tag the next runner. Use it for short shuttles with a skill at the turn.

Equipment: Cones, optional batons.

Set-up: Many teams of 4. Even teams of 4–6. Lanes about 8–12 m, home cone behind a line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. One runner at a time. Next player starts behind the home cone and goes on a high-five — not a shout from halfway.
  2. Sprint to a cone, 3 jumps or a touch, sprint back, tag.
  3. Sit when the team is done.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. Play Speed Run Relay in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Short shuttle.
  • Skill at the cone, then back.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Variations

  • Weak-foot dribble.
  • Carry a beanbag on the head.
  • Change the locomotor: walk, skip, gallop, side-slide. Running is not the only option.
  • Quality round: the team must redo a trip if the ball, bag, or pin is dropped.
  • Weaker hand or weaker foot home on the last rotation (grades 5–6).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Speed Run Relay.
  • Watch for early starts. Next runner stays behind the cone until the high-five.
  • Year plan: December, February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Out to a cone, do a named skill (3 jumps, a trap, a touch), back, tag.
  • Keep teams short so the wait is one person.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Speed Run Relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Waits behind the line for the tag.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the tag, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Hands off — does not throw at a teammate.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Runs in a lane. No diving at the line. Equipment carried, not thrown at people.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. Walk or skip. Shorter lane. Redo a trip if the skill falls apart — no extra penalty laps.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

149. Throw-Clap-Catch

Relays & stations

When: May, June · Slot: 16–25 · 1 ball · circle or pair relay lanes

Clap first, then catch. A fake that draws a clap is a miss.

Also called: Throw-clap-catch relay · Bounce and Clap · Catch Clap

Equipment: 1 ball per circle or pair

Set-up: Show the three fail states: clap on a fake, drop, catch with no clap.

How we play

  1. Throw or bounce to a player.
  2. They clap once, then catch.
  3. Miss = short task (5 marches) and they stay in. Next player.
  4. Relay version: clap-catch, then pass down the lane.

If this happens

  • Uncatchable throw: replay, no miss.
  • Whole class claps on a fake: laugh, reset, no mass out.

How a round ends. Personal clean catches, or first pair through the relay.

Cues

  • Eyes on the ball.
  • Hands underneath.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Wait behind the cone. Go on the high-five.
  • Stay in your lane. Pass on the right if you meet someone.
  • Quality over sprint — reset if the skill falls apart.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Teacher in the middle of a circle.
  • Pair relay down the gym.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Throw-clap-catch relay; Bounce and Clap; Catch Clap.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Throw-clap-catch relay for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Watches the ball into the hands.
  • Skill focus. Starts on the signal, not early; chooses a path that does not cut off another team.
  • Skill focus. Waits behind the line for their turn.
  • Character Development. No extra steps past the line; a drop is picked up and the leg continues.
  • Safety. Stays in the lane.
  • Healthy Relationships. Encourages the next runner; does not shame a drop.
Grades 1–2. No clap — toss and catch only.
Grades 3–4. Jog. Full lane. High-five hand-off. One quality redo if a pin or ball is dropped.
Grades 5–6. Skip one way, jog back — or weaker foot/hand home. A student starter on the whistle.

Safety. Pass on the right. No diving at a hoop, pin, or beanbag. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

154. Animals

Locomotor & listen

When: September, April · Slot: 0–5

Pick an animal from the habitat the teacher calls and move and sound like it. A K–2 warm-up.

How we play

  2. Each student picks an animal from that habitat and moves and sounds like it for about a minute.
  3. Change the theme and repeat.
  4. Encourage different levels (high bird, low snake) and speeds.

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-2

Equipment: None

A simple warm-up. Choose a theme, such as the jungle. Each student picks an animal from that theme (a monkey or parrot) and moves like it, with sounds, for about a minute. Then change the theme, for example to the Arctic (a penguin or seal).

  1. Call a habitat theme (jungle, arctic, farm, ocean).
  2. Each student picks an animal from that habitat and moves + makes the sound for about one minute.
  3. Change the theme and repeat. Encourage different levels (high bird, low snake) and speeds.

155. Back to Base

Locomotor & listen

When: June · Slot: 0–5 closer · Whole class · 2 min as a closer.

Play any travel game, and on the whistle everyone returns to their home base. Use it to practise listening and a quick response to a signal.

Equipment: Home hoops or spots.

Set-up: Assign bases. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Play any travel game.
  2. Whistle: everyone back to base.
  3. Last group leads a stretch, then plays again.
  4. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.
  5. Play Back to Base in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Whistle means home.
  • Last group leads a stretch — they are not out.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Must travel a named locomotor home.
  • Colour bases.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Back to Base.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Each student or pair has a home hoop.
  • On the whistle, everyone returns. Use after circus or station work.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Back to Base for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Performs the named locomotor, freeze, or shape on the signal.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Back to Base.
  • Skill focus. Last group leads a stretch — not out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Returns to the home hoop on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

157. Boom Clap Snatch

Locomotor & listen

When: December, November · Slot: 8-15 · Daly Exercise · slot 8-15.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Boom Clap Snatch

Equipment: One beanbag or cone per pair

Set-up: Pairs face each other. Object in the middle. Hands on knees.

How we play

  1. Boom = smack the floor.

  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

  • All three words. Mix the order.
  • Add dummy words.

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

  • Active Living. Stays in Boom Clap Snatch for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Boom Clap Snatch with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Boom Clap Snatch instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Boom Clap Snatch.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Boom Clap Snatch.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle. Soft tags. No throws at a face.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Two words only.
Grades 3–4. All three words. Mix the order.

158. Buffalo

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · 5–6 min.

Partners jog opposite loops while the music plays, then find each other and sit back to back when it stops. Use it for jogging intervals.

Equipment: Music. Cones to mark an inside and outside track.

Set-up: Pairs. One partner on the inside loop, one on the outside, facing opposite ways. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Music on: both jog their loop.
  2. Music off: they find each other and sit back-to-back.
  3. Last pair leads the next stretch, then everyone goes again.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Herd stays together.
  • Soft tag on the outside.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Must stay on lines.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Buffalo.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • A small group of ‘buffalo’ link elbows and jog as a herd. 2–3 hunters try to tag a free side.
  • A tag joins the herd, or the hunter becomes buffalo and a new hunter steps out.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Buffalo for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. The chain splits before it gets unsafe.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Buffalo.
  • Skill focus. Holds on without yanking.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Heads up, soft landings, freeze on the whistle. No pushing to a line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk-jog. Teacher helps pairs meet.
Grades 3–4. Jog a timed minute between stops.
Grades 5–6. Talk about race pace vs sprint on the long loop.

Safety. Pass on the outside. No racing through a sitting pair. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.
