160. Command Warm-Up

Locomotor & listen

When: February, April · Slot: 16–25 · Scatter space · no kit · a short word list

Hear the word. Do that shape or travel. Mix the order.

Also called: Red Line · The Video Camera Game · Video Camera · Captain’s Deck / Shipwreck · Shipwreck · Signals / shapes · Laps and Lines / Signals

Equipment: None · optional music

Set-up: Teach 4–6 commands before you speed up.

How we play

  1. Call a command for about 5 seconds.
  2. Call the next. Do not pattern them.
  3. A miss = skip the next command and rejoin. No extra laps.

If this happens

  • Space collisions: smaller shapes, more room.
  • Need a pulse-raiser: add music and travel the commands.

How a round ends. 2–4 minutes, then the main game.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Winter shapes.
  • Traffic speeds and red light (Rush Hour / Red Line).
  • Ship deck commands (Captain’s Deck / Shipwreck).
  • Video Camera: freeze in the pose that was called.
  • Signals / Laps and Lines: named gym lines.
  • Student caller.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Rush Hour; Red Line; The Video Camera Game; Video Camera; Captain’s Deck / Shipwreck; Shipwreck; Signals / shapes; Laps and Lines / Signals.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Red Line (PhysEdGames) · The Video Camera Game (PhysEdGames) · Captain's Coming! (Coach Meger Fitness Games)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details: The Video Camera Game · Grades K-5

Equipment: None

A simple warm-up. Call video camera actions, and students do the matching movement: play – walk; rewind – travel backwards; pause – jump; fast forward – run; stop – stop moving; slow motion – move slowly; delete – lie face down on the floor. Call them in random order. After a while, let student leaders make their own sequences and invent new actions.

  1. Teach the camera words and actions, for example: PLAY = jog forward; STOP = freeze; REWIND = run backward; FAST FORWARD = sprint; PAUSE = freeze in a pose; SLOW MOTION = exaggerated slow move; RECORD = wave an imaginary camera.
  2. Caller mixes the words. Students perform the matching action in the playing area.
  3. After a minute, switch callers so students invent sequences.
  4. Use as a K–5 warm-up.

Source · Video

More details: Red Line · Grades K-6

Equipment: Cones, balls

A commands-and-directions game with a race element. It can be used at most grade levels. Idea credit: James Barton.

  1. Use court lines or cone several parallel lines. Students start on a baseline.
  2. Teach a small set of commands (example: “red line,” “back,” “spin,” “ball”).
  3. Call commands in mixed order. Students race to the correct line or perform the action.
  4. Add a team race: first team to finish a sequence correctly scores.
  5. For older students add two-part commands or a fitness hold on arrival.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in the bout for the set time; names breath or warmth after.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the called movement (run, skip, jump, freeze).
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner without bumping others.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landings; freeze on the signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a turn.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Show three quality reps. Add a half-turn, opposite hand, or a hoop target on the last set.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

Source

161. Dash for Safety

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25 · Whole class · 5 min · colour hoops.

Call a colour, and students run to it while two taggers try to tag them. Use it to practise reacting to a call and sprinting.

Equipment: 4 coloured cones or walls.

Set-up: Class in a centre square. Two taggers outside. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Call a colour (or two). Students run there.
  2. Tagged players stretch once and re-enter on the next call.
  3. Reset taggers each minute.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Leave the centre on the colour.
  • Tagged players stretch and re-enter next call.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Two colours at once.
  • Must dribble to safety.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Dash for Safety.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Students start in a centre square. Call a colour; they dash to that hoop.
  • 2–3 taggers in the gaps. A tag is a stretch, then you join the next dash.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Dash for Safety for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Crosses on the call, not after a wait.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Dash for Safety.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Tagged player re-enters next wave — no last-one-standing.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. Dribble a sport ball to the colour.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

162. Daytime / Nighttime

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 4 min.

Students travel on “Daytime” and freeze in a strong shape on “Nighttime.” Use it to practise stillness and gymnastic shapes.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: General space. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. “Daytime” = travel.
  2. “Nighttime” = freeze in a strong shape.
  3. Look for stillness, not tricks.
  4. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.
  5. Play Daytime / Nighttime in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Day = move. Night = freeze.
  • Eyes stay open at night.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Dawn = slow motion.
  • Student owl watches instead of the teacher.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Daytime / Nighttime.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Teacher or a student faces away and calls ‘daytime’ (jog) or ‘nighttime’ (freeze).
  • If you move at night, jog to a wall and back, then re-enter. No sitting out.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Daytime / Nighttime for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named locomotor or shape.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Daytime / Nighttime.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Changes movement on the first signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

163. Elements Sprint

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class in open space · 4–8 minutes · freeze on the signal, then the next command.

Call Fire, Earth or Water, or an animal that belongs there, and students run to the matching wall. Use it to practise listening and a quick sprint.

Equipment: Three wall signs: Fire, Earth, Water.

Set-up: Students in the middle. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Call “Fire,” “Earth,” or “Water,” or an animal that belongs there (fish → water).
  2. Last group to the wall does 5 jumping jacks and stays in.
  3. Students may take a turn as the caller.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Hear the word.
  • Hands up at the wall.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Two walls for 1–2.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Elements Sprint.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Fire, Earth, Water — or an animal. Late group: 5 jacks, stay in.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Elements Sprint for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Changes direction on the call.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Elements Sprint.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Stops with hands up.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. Add “air” as the centre freeze.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

164. European Rhythmic Running

Locomotor & listen

When: February · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25 · Whole class · 4–6 min.

Students change their locomotor skill or level on each 8-count phrase. Use it to practise moving to a steady beat.

Equipment: Whistle, clap, or music (or a chanted 8-count).

Set-up: Scatter in general space. Practise a walk on the beat. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Teacher (or a student) keeps an 8-count.
  2. Change locomotor or level on the phrase: walk, skip, slide, freeze shape.
  3. Silence is a freeze.
  4. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.
  5. Play European Rhythmic Running in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Match the beat.
  • Soft feet.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Levels on the phrase.
  • Add a clap pattern.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in European Rhythmic Running.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Jog a circle or a figure-8 on the whistle or music.
  • Change: walk, skip, side-slide, freeze on a stop in the music.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in European Rhythmic Running for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Jumps or steps the rope; quiet land.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in European Rhythmic Running.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Rope stays low; no whipping.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. Floor ropes or court lines only — no benches. One walker on a line. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

165. Fishing

Locomotor & listen

When: November · Slot: 8-15

Hook cones with a noodle and bring them home.

Equipment: Pool noodle; scattered cones

Set-up: Scatter cones. Pair shares one noodle and a home hoop.

How we play

  1. One hooks a cone, returns, passes the noodle.
  2. Continue until cones are gone.
  3. Freeze on the whistle.
Grades 1–2. Colour-only fish.
Grades 3–4. Seated fisher plus running partner.
Grades 5–6. Must hook through the top hole.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle.

166. Follow the Leader

Locomotor & listen

When: December, April · Slot: 0–5

Copy a locomotor or shape in a small line.

Equipment: Optional whistle, clap or music.

Set-up: Lines of 4.

How we play

  1. Leader travels or holds a shape.
  2. Switch leader every 20 seconds.
Grades 1–2. Teacher starts as leader.
Grades 3–4. Student leaders.
Grades 5–6. Must include a level change.

Safety. Leave space. No tumbling off mats.

167. Four Corners

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 16–25 · Four labelled corners · class in the centre

Get to a corner on the call. Everyone stays in the game.

Also called: Four Corners Stay-In

Equipment: Four labelled corners (numbers or colours)

Set-up: Label 1–4 or by colour. Pupils start centre.

How we play

  1. Call a travel style and a corner: ‘Skip to blue.’
  2. Everyone travels. The named corner (or last arrivals, your choice) does 5–8 jumping jacks or a stretch.
  3. Back to centre. New call.

If this happens

  • Sliding into corners: send them back to travel again.
  • Two corners called: they must choose one.
  • Use the last call to line up.

How a round ends. 3–6 minutes, then into the main game.

Cues

  • Walk to a corner.
  • Called corner does a skill, then stays.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Numbers.
  • Colours.
  • Maths: even corners only.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Four Corners Stay-In.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Four Corners Stay-In for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Gets to a corner before the count ends.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Four Corners Stay-In.
  • Skill focus. Does the skill and stays in.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Heads up, soft landings, freeze on the whistle. No pushing to a line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. No diving at a corner. First two feet on the spot keep it. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

168. Group Numbers

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 3–4 min.

Call a number, and students form groups of that size in 5 seconds, with leftover players joining you. Use it to practise listening and including everyone.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Jog in space. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Call a number. Students form groups of that size in 5 seconds.
  2. Leftover players join the teacher — they are never out.
  3. Jog again and call a new number.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Jog until the number.
  • Leftovers join the teacher — they are not out.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Must hold a shape in the group.
  • Call ‘plus one muscle’ and they add a stretch.
  • Travel 20–30 seconds first (run, skip, hop), then freeze and form groups of the called number. Leftovers join the teacher — never out.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Group Numbers.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Jog in space. Call a number. Students make groups of that size in 5 seconds.
  • Leftovers make a group with you. Then jog again.

Video. The Number Game (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details: The Number Game · Grades K-6

Equipment: None

A warm-up or team-building game. Students spread out and run (or skip, hop, gallop, spin) for 20–30 seconds. Call a number (for example, “Six!”), and students quickly form groups of that size. Anyone not in a group does that many jumping jacks or another exercise, then play again. It works well with music and with large groups. You can follow it with a discussion about inclusion and how it feels to be left out.

  1. Students spread out and travel for 20–30 seconds (run, skip, hop, gallop, spin).
  2. Teacher yells a number (“SIX!”). Students instantly form groups of that size.
  3. Anyone left out does that many jumping jacks (or a chosen exercise) and rejoins.
  4. Travel again and call a new number. Use music if you like.
  5. After play, a short talk on inclusion and how it feels to be left out / invited in.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Group Numbers for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Finds a group or a spot without diving.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Group Numbers.
  • Skill focus. Leftovers stay in — they join the teacher or the next round.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Heads up, soft landings, freeze on the whistle. No pushing to a line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Up to 6. Add a shape the group must make.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

169. Guess Who

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 8-15 · Daly Exercise · slot 8-15.

Notice who took the ball while the circle keeps moving. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Guess Who

Equipment: One soft ball

Set-up: One pupil in the middle. Others walk a circle.

How we play

  1. Circle walks.
  2. Teacher points to one walker who takes the ball.
  3. Middle player guesses. Correct = swap.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Allow two guesses.
  • One guess. Keep the circle moving.
  • Two middle players.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Guess Who for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Guess Who with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Guess Who instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Guess Who.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Guess Who.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Allow two guesses.
Grades 3–4. One guess. Keep the circle moving.
Grades 5–6. Two middle players.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

170. Here, There, Everywhere

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 4 min.

Students move toward you on “Here,” where you point on “There,” and scatter on “Everywhere.” Use it to practise listening and changing direction.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Teacher is the pointer. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Spread out so you cannot touch a neighbour with an arm stretched. First command is a walk.
  2. Here = move toward the teacher. There = move where they point. Everywhere = scatter.
  3. Change locomotor each round.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Here = toward the teacher. There = away. Everywhere = scatter.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Add ‘home’ = a colour hoop.
  • Student caller.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Here, There, Everywhere.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Point and call. Change locomotor each round (walk, skip, side-slide).
  • Freeze on a drum. No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Here, There, Everywhere for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named locomotor or shape.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Here, There, Everywhere.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Changes movement on the first signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. Students take the pointer role.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

171. Hoop Twister

Locomotor & listen

When: February · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25

Musical hoops meets Twister: when the music stops, put the called body part in a hoop of the called colour.

Equipment: Coloured hoops, music

Set-up: Scatter coloured hoops like a giant Twister board.

How we play

  1. Music on: travel around the hoops with the skill you name.
  2. Music off: call a body part and a colour (“right hand, blue”). Put that body part in a matching hoop.
  3. Sharing a hoop is allowed. Anyone who cannot get in does 5 reps and rejoins.
  4. Remove 1–2 hoops each round.

Video. Hoop Twister (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-4

Equipment: Hoops, music

A mix of musical chairs and the Twister board game. Idea credit: Deric Hafer.

  1. Scatter coloured hoops like a giant Twister board.
  2. Music on: travel around the hoops with the locomotor skill you name.
  3. Music off: call a body part + colour (“right hand, blue”). Students place that body part in a matching hoop.
  4. Sharing a hoop is allowed. Anyone who cannot get in does 5 reps and rejoins.
  5. Remove 1–2 hoops each round.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Teacher calls. Two or three commands only. Walk before you jog.
Grades 3–4. Add commands and speed. Let a student call one round.
Grades 5–6. Add fitness moves or a sequence to remember. Students lead.

Safety. Spread out to arm's-length space. Look where you go. Freeze on the whistle. Step into hoops — no diving.

172. Human Bop-It

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · 4 min.

Call an action on the beat (kick it, twist it, spin it, pull it, bop it), and the class does it. Use it to practise listening and quick reactions.

Equipment: None. Optional music.

Set-up: Scatter. Teach the five actions: kick it, twist it, spin it, pull it, bop it. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Spread out so you cannot touch a neighbour with an arm stretched. First command is a walk.
  2. Caller names an action on the beat. Class does it in place or travelling.
  3. Speed up. Wrong action = a stretch, then rejoin. No elimination.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Hear the action, then do it.
  • Freeze between calls.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Add ‘repeat’ to do the last action twice.
  • Opposite actions (twist the other way).
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Human Bop-It.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Call Bop-It actions as locomotor or shapes: twist, pull (high stretch), spin, bop (jump), pass it (high-five a neighbour).
  • Speed up. A wrong move is 3 jumping jacks, stay in.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Human Bop-It for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named locomotor or shape.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Human Bop-It.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Changes movement on the first signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. Syncopated calls. Student DJs.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

173. Jumping Beans

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 4 min.

Call a bean, and students change how they move: jump, jog, wiggle or freeze. Use it to practise listening and locomotor skills.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Scatter in general space. Teach 4 beans. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Spread out so you cannot touch a neighbour with an arm stretched. First command is a walk.
  2. Jumping bean = jump. Runner bean = jog. Jelly bean = wiggle-travel. Frozen bean = still shape.
  3. Call a new bean every 10–15 seconds.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Show the bean, then freeze on ‘frozen bean.’
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Students invent a bean.
  • Add a drum for the freeze.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Jumping Beans.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Jumping bean, runner bean, jelly bean, broad bean (wide shape), frozen bean, chilli bean (fast jog), coffee bean (grind / small circle).
  • Change every 8–10 seconds. Frozen bean is a still shape for 3 seconds.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Jumping Beans for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named locomotor or shape.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Jumping Beans.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Changes movement on the first signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

174. Land and Sea

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 4 min · one long line.

Call land or sea, and students jump to that side of the line. Use it to practise body control and listening.

Equipment: A centre line.

Set-up: Name one side land, one side sea. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Spread out so you cannot touch a neighbour with an arm stretched. First command is a walk.
  2. Call land or sea. Students jump to that side.
  3. Mix hop, turn, or freeze.
  4. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.
  5. Play Land and Sea in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Two feet on the correct side.
  • Mix hop, turn, freeze.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Add ‘shark’ = sit with feet up.
  • Two lines = a river.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Land and Sea.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • A line is the shore. One side is land, the other is sea.
  • Jump to the called side. Late jumpers do 5 jumps and stay in.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Land and Sea for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named locomotor or shape.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Land and Sea.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Changes movement on the first signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. Student callers. Fake calls.

Safety. Two-foot landings. Space along the line. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

175. Mirror Mirror

Locomotor & listen

When: September, February · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25

Two groups run opposite ways to music. When it stops, one group freezes in a pose and the other mirrors it.

Equipment: Music

Set-up: Two equal groups. Group A outside the basketball-court line, Group B inside.

How we play

  1. Music on: the outside group runs one way, the inside group runs the other way.
  2. Music off: the outside group freezes in any pose. Each inside player stands 1–2 metres in front of a frozen player and mirrors the pose.
  3. Swap roles. An extra player joins a group if numbers are odd.
  4. For older students, use fitness poses (plank, lunge, squat).

Video. Mirror Mirror (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 1-5

Equipment: Music

Use it as a stand-alone game, a warm-up or a fitness activity. Divide players into 2 equal groups, one outside the basketball court line and one inside. When the music plays, the outside group runs one way and the inside group runs the other way. When the music stops, the outside group freezes in any pose. Each inside player stands in front of a frozen player (1–2 metres apart) and copies the pose. Then the groups switch. With an odd number, let one player join either group. For older students, use fitness poses such as a plank, side plank, lunge or squat. Idea credit: Anne Guilmaine.

  1. Two equal groups. Group A stands outside the basketball court line; Group B stands inside.
  2. Music on: outside group runs one direction, inside group runs the opposite direction.
  3. Music off: the outside group FREEZES in any pose. Inside players each stand 1–2 metres in front of a frozen player and mirror the pose.
  4. Swap roles (outside becomes inside). If numbers are odd, allow one extra player to join a group.
  5. For older students require fitness poses (plank, lunge, squat).

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Teacher calls. Two or three commands only. Walk before you jog.
Grades 3–4. Add commands and speed. Let a student call one round.
Grades 5–6. Add fitness moves or a sequence to remember. Students lead.

Safety. Spread out to arm's-length space. Look where you go. Freeze on the whistle. Inside group runs on the inside lane only.

176. Mongoose Tag

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class in open space · 4–8 minutes · freeze on the signal, then the next command.

Call a body part, and partners run in, match those parts, then return to the start. Use it to practise partner reaction and body-part awareness.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Pairs. A centre line. Teacher has a list of body parts. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Spread out so you cannot touch a neighbour with an arm stretched. First command is a walk.
  2. On a called body part (elbow, knee, back), pairs run in, match those parts, then return to the start.
  3. The last pair does 5 jumping jacks and plays the next round — they are not out.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Match the body part.
  • Soft taps.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Add a locomotor between calls.
  • Students call.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Mongoose Tag.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Pairs face. Teacher calls two body parts (knee-elbow, toe-shoulder). Partners touch those parts together and freeze.
  • Then jog and find a new partner on the drum.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Mongoose Tag for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the called body parts.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Mongoose Tag.
  • Skill focus. A student may show space instead of a touch.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Heads up, soft landings, freeze on the whistle. No pushing to a line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk in. Two body parts only.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

177. Partner Orienteering

Locomotor & listen

When: May, June · Slot: 16-25 · Daly Exercise · slot 16-25.

Map-to-ground. The pair stays together. Both hold the map. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Partner Orienteering

Equipment: Simple maps; controls; record cards

Set-up: Map matches the real space. Show how to turn the map.

How we play

  1. Orient the map, walk to the first control.
  2. Record the answer. Swap who leads.
  3. Pairs stay together the whole time.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Colour-coded first loop.
  • Full simple course.
  • More controls. No splitting up.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Partner Orienteering for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Partner Orienteering with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Partner Orienteering instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Partner Orienteering.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Partner Orienteering.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Colour-coded first loop.
Grades 3–4. Full simple course.
Grades 5–6. More controls. No splitting up.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

178. Rubber Band

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 4–5 min · a long rope or elastic, or just a circle of hands.

Students travel away from the circle on the first signal and sprint back on the second. Use it to practise locomotor skills and reaction.

Equipment: A centre circle of spots or a centre cone.

Set-up: Class stands on a circle facing out. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. First signal: travel away using the named locomotor.
  2. Second signal: sprint back, jump, and shout.
  3. Repeat with a new locomotor.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. Play Rubber Band in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • The band expands and shrinks on the call.
  • Do not let go.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Add a shape in the middle.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Rubber Band.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Class holds a rope circle or joins hands. On ‘stretch,’ step back; on ‘snap,’ step in; on ‘travel,’ the whole band jogs.
  • If the band breaks, freeze, rejoin, continue.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Rubber Band for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Finds a group or a spot without diving.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Rubber Band.
  • Skill focus. Leftovers stay in — they join the teacher or the next round.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Heads up, soft landings, freeze on the whistle. No pushing to a line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Eyes up on the return. Leave a gap at the circle. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

179. Rush Hour

Locomotor & listen

When: September, February · Slot: 0–5

A try-not-to-crash traffic game. Students are cars that change speed and skill on the call.

Equipment: None

Set-up: Students are cars in their own space. Review “look where you go” and no crashing.

How we play

  1. Call a locomotor skill and a speed: slow traffic, city, highway.
  2. Add rules: red light freeze, green light go, reverse, flat tire (hop), out of gas (sit or plank), carpool (join a partner).
  3. If two cars touch, both do 5 jumping jacks and rejoin traffic.
  4. Play 3–5 minutes as a warm-up.

  3. Add rules: red light freeze, green light go, reverse, flat tire (hop), out of gas (sit or plank), carpool (join a partner).
  4. If two cars touch, both do 5 jumping jacks and re-enter traffic.
  5. Play 3–5 minutes as a primary warm-up.

181. Simon Says Stay-In

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class in open space · 4–8 minutes · freeze on the signal, then the next command.

Students do the action only after “Simon says,” and a miss is 5 jumps or a stretch, not an out. Use it to practise listening.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Scattered in personal space. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Do the action only when you hear “Simon says.”
  2. A miss: 5 jumps or a stretch, then keep playing.
  3. Let a student lead the last minute.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Only if you hear Simon says.
  • A miss is 5 jumps.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Slow locomotor cues for 1–2.
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Simon Says Stay-In.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Keep playing. Student leads the last minute.

Video. Simon Says (The PE Teacher)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Simon Says Stay-In for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named skill.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Simon Says Stay-In.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Starts and stops on the phrase.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Slow cues. Locomotor and shapes.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

182. Sneak & React

Locomotor & listen

When: September, May · Slot: 0–5

Tiptoe toward the leader on the sneak cue; react fast on the react cue. Reaction timing, no equipment.

Also called: Knock and Run

Equipment: None

Set-up: Class scattered, facing a leader at one end.

How we play

  1. On the sneak cue, tiptoe forward.
  2. On the react cue (clap, colour, “TURN”), sprint back or change action.
  3. Mix in freeze, plank, jump-turn, partner high-five.
  4. A false start is 3 jumping jacks, then rejoin. Let older students lead the cues.

Variations

  • Knock and Run (shoulder-tap chase): Mark a start line and a far line about 15 m apart, shorter for Grades 1–2. The first player walks to the far line and faces the wall. The next player creeps up quietly and gives one light tap on the shoulder, then turns and sprints back to the start line while the tapped player turns and chases. A two-finger tag on the back before the line means the two swap roles, or the tagged player does a quick stretch, then rejoins. Run through the line and slow down. Both players then walk to the back of a different line.

Video. Sneak & React (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-8

Equipment: None

A reaction-timing game for all ages. Use it as an instant activity, a warm-up or a stand-alone game. No equipment needed.

  1. Class faces a leader in scattered formation.
  2. On a sneak cue, students tiptoe forward. On a react cue (clap, colour, “TURN”) they sprint back or change action.
  3. Mix in freeze, plank, jump-turn, partner high-five.
  4. False start = 3 jumping jacks and rejoin. Let older students lead cue sequences.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Teacher calls. Two or three commands only. Walk before you jog.
Grades 3–4. Add commands and speed. Let a student call one round.
Grades 5–6. Add fitness moves or a sequence to remember. Students lead.

Safety. Spread out to arm's-length space. Look where you go. Freeze on the whistle.

Source

183. Video Game

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · 5 min.

Call media cues such as play, pause, rewind and fast-forward, and students change how they move. Use it for creative movement and listening.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Teach play, rewind, fast-forward, pause, eject, slow-motion. Use the basketball lines as the boundary; cones mark any safe zone. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Spread out so you cannot touch a neighbour with an arm stretched. First command is a walk.
  2. Students travel. On each cue they change how they move.
  3. Pause is a still shape. Eject is a jump to a new spot.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. If you miss a signal, skip the next one and rejoin — no extra laps as punishment.

Cues

  • Teacher is the controller.
  • A, B, start, pause mean taught actions.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Variations

  • Two-player: half the class is player 1.
  • Add a boss (a tagger) on ‘level 2.’
  • Whisper round, then a music round. Same skills, different energy.
  • Let a student call two commands (teacher stays on freeze and safety).
  • Add a shape at the freeze: tuck, star, or stork.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Video Game.
  • Watch for crashing on a freeze. Practise a walk-freeze before any sprint command.
  • Year plan: September, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Teach 4–5 button actions: A = jump, B = spin, start = jog, pause = freeze, select = change locomotor.
  • Call a sequence (A-A-B). Students play the combo.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Video Game for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Shows the named locomotor or shape.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Video Game.
  • Skill focus. Listens with the group; nobody is ever out.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Changes movement on the first signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. Student narrators stack two cues.

Safety. Fast-forward is quick feet, not a sprint into people. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

184. Volcanoes and Ice-Cream Cones

Locomotor & listen

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25 · Two teams · many scattered cones

Flip every cone to your shape before the whistle.

Also called: On/Off

Equipment: Many scattered cones

Set-up: Scatter cones mixed upright (ice-cream / volcano up) and upside down. Team Up vs Team Down. No guarding a cone with your body.

How we play

  1. On go, flip cones to your state. Hands only.
  2. Whistle. Hands on heads. Count each shape.
  3. Switch jobs and play again.

If this happens

  • Kicked cone: put it back as it was.
  • Held so the other team cannot touch it: let go.
  • Count dispute: teacher counts.

How a round ends. Most cones in your state after 45–90 seconds.

Cues

  • Flip, don’t kick.
  • No guarding a cone.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Move the way I named (walk, skip, gallop) — not a race.
  • Stop in a balanced shape: two feet, knees soft.
  • Give neighbours a hoop of space.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Progression: ball on top vs ball underneath.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: On/Off (year-round name).
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Volcanoes and Ice-Cream Cones for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Finds a group or a spot without diving.
  • Skill focus. Finds an open pathway on the go signal; does not cut a neighbour in Volcanoes and Ice-Cream Cones.
  • Skill focus. Leftovers stay in — they join the teacher or the next round.
  • Character Development. Freezes honestly on the signal even if it is fun to keep running.
  • Safety. Heads up, soft landings, freeze on the whistle. No pushing to a line.
  • Healthy Relationships. Moves around people, not through them; helps a classmate who missed the cue.
Grades 1–2. Walk first. Two commands only (go and freeze). Teacher models every shape.
Grades 3–4. Add skip, gallop, and a shape at the freeze. Students may suggest one command.
Grades 5–6. A student DJ or caller for two minutes. Teacher keeps freeze and safety.

Safety. Give a hoop of space. Freeze means still feet — not a last-second shove. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

185. Beach Ball or Scarf Keep-Up

Cooperative & circus

When: March, December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Circles of 4–8 · one beach ball or scarf per circle · optional noodles or rulers

Keep one beach ball or scarf up. Count hits. A drop restarts the count, not the person.

Also called: Balloon Keep-Up · Balloon Keep-Ups · Balloon Keep-Up station

Equipment: One beach ball or juggling scarf per circle · optional noodles or rulers

Set-up: Circles with arm’s-length space. One beach ball or scarf each.

How we play

  1. Name the shared goal out loud — you are not chasing a winner between circles unless you say so.
  2. Toss the beach ball or scarf in. Soft taps only.
  3. Count hits aloud. Floor = restart that circle’s count.
  4. Play 3–6 minutes.

If this happens

  • Hard swat: sit one restart, then rejoin.
  • Sticks clash: widen the circle.
  • Ball or scarf leaves the circle: retrieve and restart that count.

How a round ends. Highest consecutive count, or first circle to 20.

Cues

  • Call it.
  • A drop restarts the count, not the player.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or ball.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Hands only.
  • Noodle/ruler only.
  • Second beach ball or scarf.
  • Seated chairs.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Balloon Keep-Up; Balloon Keep-Ups; Balloon Keep-Up station.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Balloon Keep Up (CIRAOntario)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Beach Ball or Scarf Keep-Up for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Catch, then throw — call it.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses a name or ‘mine’ before the send.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Class record. Weak hand only.

Safety. No heading hard balls. Latex allergy: use a beach ball. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

186. Birthday Lineup

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · silent.

Students line up silently by birthday, from January to December. Use it to practise communicating without talking.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: One long wall or line. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Silent. Show the month with fingers, then the day.
  2. January is the left end of the gym, December the right.
  3. When the line is still, check from one end. Celebrate the mix-ups, then try once more.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.

Cues

  • Fingers for the month.
  • Walk to the line.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Teacher may whisper for 1–2.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Birthday Lineup.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • January left, December right. Check once. Try again.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Birthday Lineup for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Stays silent through the check.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses gestures, not a shove.
Grades 1–2. Teacher may whisper a month to a stuck pair.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Add a silent ‘same week’ huddle.

Safety. Ask before you pull a hoop, rope, or teammate. Stop if hair, glasses, or a wrist is caught. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

187. Blindfold Walk

Cooperative & circus

When: April, December · Slot: 16-25 · Daly Exercise · slot 16-25.

Walk a rope path with a talking spotter. Closed eyes are legal. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Blindfold Walk

Equipment: One blindfold per team; ropes laid flat

Set-up: Ropes to step over. Spotter beside, not behind.

How we play

  1. Walker covers eyes at the start.
  2. Foot on a rope = stop, return, no point.
  3. Clean crossing = 1. Swap jobs.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Closed eyes or a visor. Elbow guide allowed.
  • Voice cues only. No dragging.
  • Silent spotter, then voice.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

  • Active Living. Stays in Blindfold Walk for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Blindfold Walk with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Blindfold Walk instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Blindfold Walk.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss or a line in Blindfold Walk.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Closed eyes or a visor. Elbow guide allowed.
Grades 3–4. Voice cues only. No dragging.

189. Bridges & Rivers

Cooperative & circus

When: April · Slot: 0–5

On the call, half the class makes bridges or rivers and the other half crawls under or jumps over.

Equipment: None · optional music

Set-up: Half the class are structures, half are travellers. Scattered in open space.

How we play

  1. On “BRIDGES,” structures make a stable bridge (hands and feet down, back flat). Travellers crawl under.
  2. On “RIVERS,” structures lie or crouch low. Travellers jump over.
  3. Teacher or a student caller switches the call. Play to music.
  4. Swap jobs so everyone travels and everyone holds.

Video. Bridges & Rivers (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-4

Equipment: None

Players form bridges or rivers and jump or crawl depending on the call. It works well with music. Make half the players bridges and half rivers. When the music starts (or on a signal), everyone moves around. When it stops, call “bridges” or “rivers.” On “bridges,” bridges make a bridge with their bodies and rivers crawl under as many as they can. On “rivers,” rivers lie flat and bridges jump over as many as they can. Restart the music and repeat. Switch roles after a while.

  1. On “BRIDGES,” some students make a stable bridge (hands and feet on the floor, back flat). Others are rivers and crawl under.
  2. On “RIVERS,” some students lie or crouch as a low river. Others jump over.
  3. Teacher (or a student caller) switches the call. Play to music.
  4. Rotate who is the structure and who is travelling so everyone jumps and everyone holds.
  5. Cue safe landings and no collapsing on a crawler.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–5. Teacher shows it first. No timer.
Grades 3–4. Groups of 6–8. Time trial against your own best.
Grades 5–6. Whole class or team vs team. Add a rule (no talking, fewer touches).

Safety. Stable bridges only. Safe landings. Nobody collapses onto a crawler; jump over the legs, not the head.

Source

191. Caterpillar Walk

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class or groups of 6–10 · 6–10 minutes · celebrate the group task, not a winner.

Teams of 5–7 move as one line along a cone path, holding waists or sharing hoops. Use it to practise moving together and taking turns as leader.

Equipment: Cones. Optional shared hoop.

Set-up: Teams of 5–7 in a file. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  3. Trade the leader each lap.
  4. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.
  5. Play Caterpillar Walk in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

  • Freeze if it breaks.

  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

  • Shoulders for 1–2.

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Caterpillar Walk.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Hold waist or a shared hoop. Walk the cones. New leader each lap.

Alberta PEW outcomes

  • Active Living. Stays in Caterpillar Walk for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the line’s speed.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Rejoins after a break.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without pulling equipment away.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Add a hoop the line must step through.

Safety. Ask before you pull a hoop, rope, or teammate. Stop if hair, glasses, or a wrist is caught. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

Cooperative & circus

Place a cone in the hoop and still catch the bib.
