193. Copy Chain

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Lines or circles of 6–10 · optional music

Copy what you saw, then add one move. A mix-up restarts the phrase.

Also called: Follow the Leader · Dance Whispers · Dance Chain · Dance Tag · Disco Stu’s Dance Tag

Equipment: Optional music

Set-up: Agree: mix-up restarts, nobody sits the period. Keep phrases to 4–8 counts.

How we play

  1. Person 1 shows one move.
  2. Person 2 copies it and adds one. Continue.
  3. If the order is lost, restart from that person. Celebrate mutations.

If this happens

  • Phrase becomes a 30-second routine: cut it down.
  • Someone shouts the moves ahead: start again silently.

How a round ends. Longest clean chain, or likeness score out of 5 if you passed it as a whisper.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.
  • One extra person in a hoop during a swap — no shoving out.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Whisper down a line, last person performs.
  • Mirror Mirror: freeze in a pose, partner matches.
  • Dance Tag: start a clear dance before the tag to be safe.
  • Follow the Leader: one leader, class copies, swap often.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Pass It On; Mirror Mirror; Follow the Leader; Dance Whispers; Dance Chain; Dance Tag; Disco Stu’s Dance Tag.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Pass It On for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. The group finishes the task together.
  • Character Development. Restarts after a mistake without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without grabbing.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups design one extra rule that still includes everyone, then teach it to another group.

Safety. Ask before you pull a hoop, rope, or teammate. Stop if hair, glasses, or a wrist is caught. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

194. Elves, Giants, Wizards

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Two teams · 5–6 min · a centre line and two homes.

Teams huddle and choose a pose, and the winning pose chases the other team home. Use it for a team decision and a short chase with no elimination.

Equipment: Cones for two team lines and two safe zones.

Set-up: Two teams on their lines. Teach the three poses and who beats whom: elf beats wizard, wizard beats giant, giant beats elf. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Teams huddle and choose one pose. They walk to the middle, chant twice, then show.
  2. The winning pose chases. The losing team runs home.
  3. Tagged players join the other team. Play several quick rounds.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.

Cues

  • Show the shape together.
  • The winning shape chases; tagged players switch teams.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Teacher picks the shape for Grade 1.
  • Must skip home.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Elves, Giants, Wizards.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Like Rock-Paper-Scissors as a team: elves beat giants, giants beat wizards, wizards beat elves. Teach the three shapes.
  • Teams huddle, choose one shape, meet at the centre, show on a count of 3.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Elves, Giants, Wizards for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Tagged player re-enters or switches — nobody sits out.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Moves only when their name or shape wins.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without pulling equipment away.
Grades 1–2. Walk the chase. Teacher may choose the first pose with them.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups design one extra rule that still includes everyone, then teach it to another group.

Safety. Soft tag. Stop at the safe line. (Use this standard PE version — not a throwing game.). No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

195. Flip Flop

Cooperative & circus

When: December, April · Slot: 8-15

Half the class are tunnels and bridges; the other half travel, then swap.

Equipment: None (bodies as kit)

Set-up: Tunnels (hands-knees) and bridges (pike). Gaps between.

How we play

  1. Travellers crawl through and jump or step over.
  2. Swap and rebuild.
  3. Freeze on the whistle.
Grades 1–2. Tunnels only.
Grades 3–4. Walk around instead of over.
Grades 5–6. Theme the course.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle.

196. Hoop Hut

Cooperative & circus

When: February, December · Slot: 16-25 · Daly Exercise · slot 16-25.

Build a six-hoop hut from a picture and carry it to a cone. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Hoop Hut

Equipment: Six hoops per group; a picture; a cone 10-15 m away

Set-up: Show a finished hut first.

How we play

  1. Group builds to match the picture.
  2. When it stands, lift and walk toward the cone.
  3. Collapse = carry back, rebuild.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Fewer hoops. Slide instead of a full lift.
  • Six hoops. Slow walk.
  • Picture only. Time cap.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Hoop Hut for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Hoop Hut with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Hoop Hut instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Hoop Hut.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Hoop Hut.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Fewer hoops. Slide instead of a full lift.
Grades 3–4. Six hoops. Slow walk.
Grades 5–6. Picture only. Time cap.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

197. Hoop Pass

Cooperative & circus

When: September, December · Slot: 0–5

Hold hands in a circle and pass a hoop all the way around without letting go.

Equipment: One hoop per group

Set-up: Groups hold hands in a circle or line. A hoop starts on one person’s arm.

How we play

  1. Pass the hoop around without letting go of hands — step through and wriggle it over.
  2. If the chain breaks, restart the hoop at the break.
  3. Time trials, team vs team, or one giant class circle.
  4. Talk about communication between rounds.

Video. Hoop Pass (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-8

Equipment: Hoop

A team-building game. A group holds hands in a circle or line and passes a hoop from player to player without letting go. Play as a timed trial, team against team, or as one large group.

  1. A group holds hands in a circle or line. A hoop starts on one person’s arm.
  2. Pass the hoop around the group WITHOUT letting go of hands — step through, wriggle it over bodies.
  3. Time trials, team vs team, or one giant class circle all work.
  4. If the chain breaks, restart the hoop at the break. Talk about communication.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–5. Teacher shows it first. No timer.
Grades 3–4. Groups of 6–8. Time trial against your own best.
Grades 5–6. Whole class or team vs team. Add a rule (no talking, fewer touches).

Safety. Agree the freeze signal and a safe way to stop. No pulling or dragging a teammate.

198. Human Knot

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class or groups of 6–10 · 6–10 minutes · celebrate the group task, not a winner.

In circles of 6–8, students hold hands across the circle and work together to untangle it. Use it with Grade 3 and up to practise problem solving, and ask for consent first.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Circles of 6–8. Grade 3+. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Each student holds two different hands across the circle.
  2. Step, turn, and duck until the circle is open. Do not let go unless someone says stop.
  3. A student may leave at any time and become a coach.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.

Cues

  • Say no any time.
  • No lifts.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Skip for 1–2; use hoop-pass.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Human Knot.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Two different hands. Untangle. A leaver becomes a coach.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Human Knot for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Tries a second plan after a stuck moment.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Stops when someone says no.
Grades 1–2. Skip. Use hoop-pass instead.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups of 8. Time a second try.

Safety. No lifts. Stop if a wrist or neck is twisted. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

200. Invent-a-game

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Groups of 4 · 15–20 min across a lesson.

Groups of four write three fair rules for a new game and teach it to the class. Use it to practise co-operation and fair play.

Equipment: Whatever the group chooses from a teacher-approved pile.

Set-up: Groups of 4. One clipboard or whiteboard. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Write: how to start, how to score or tag, how to stay safe and included.
  2. Teach in 60 seconds. Class plays for 4 minutes.
  3. Ask: did everyone get a turn?
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.

Cues

  • Start, score, safety — three sentences.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Must include a send.
  • Must include a freeze.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Invent-a-game.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Give each group a sport ball and 4 cones. They invent a game with a start, a way to score, and one safety rule.
  • Play-test for 2 minutes, change one rule, then teach another group.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Invent-a-game for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Can say start, score, and safety in three sentences.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Teaches those three things to another group in a short turn.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without pulling equipment away.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Add an equipment rule and a referee.

Safety. Teacher vetoes any elimination or hard-throw rule. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

201. Object Toss mixer

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · 5 min · mixed balls, beanbags, and rings.

Students say a name and toss a scarf, adding objects until several are in the air. Use it to practise names, tossing and catching.

Equipment: Scarves, then beanbags.

Set-up: One circle, or two if the class is large. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Start with one scarf. Say a name and toss.
  2. Add a second object, then a third.
  3. Drops are part of circus — restart that object, do not stop the game.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. Play Object Toss mixer in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Match the send to the object.
  • Call the name before you throw.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Non-dominant hand.
  • Must send to a moving partner.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Object Toss mixer.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Beanbags, light balls, rings in a middle pile.
  • On the whistle, take one, find a partner, complete 5 sends, switch objects.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Object Toss mixer for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the send to the object.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Moves on the call, not before.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without pulling equipment away.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups design one extra rule that still includes everyone, then teach it to another group.

Safety. Toss to a spot just above your head, not at faces. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

202. Parachute popcorn / dome / cat-and-mouse

Cooperative & circus

When: October, December · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class on the chute · 8–10 min.

Run the core parachute activities: waves, the dome and a chase under the parachute. Use it to practise working together on a count.

Equipment: Parachute or king-size sheet, beanbags.

Set-up: Everyone has a colour handle. Practise a count of three to lift. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

  • Bend knees for a dome.

  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Number games under the chute.
  • Mushroom freeze.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Parachute popcorn / dome / cat-and-mouse.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: October, December — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Ripples: small waves. Popcorn: light balls or beanbags on the chute — keep them on. Dome: step in, lift, sit on the edge if you taught it.
  • Cat and mouse: one student on top (walking), one underneath — only if the dome is well-held and you have taught it. Skip for Grade 1 if the chute is chaotic.

  • Active Living. Stays in Parachute popcorn / dome / cat-and-mouse for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.

Grades 5–6. Students count the lift. Pop to a target hoop.

203. Pass It On

Cooperative & circus

When: December, February · Slot: 16–25

Teams lie in a line and move every object from one end to the other using only their feet.

Equipment: Hoops, a mix of soft items (balls, beanbags, noodles)

Set-up: Teams lie on their backs in a line from one end of the gym. Objects start at one end; a hoop at the far end.

How we play

  1. Pass only with your feet — no hands.
  2. Move every object down the line into the hoop at the far end.
  3. An item that touches a hand or the floor restarts from the beginning.
  4. First team to move everything wins. Mix item sizes to change the difficulty.

Video. Pass It On (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 2-8

Equipment: Hoops, various items

A team-building game. Teams pass objects from one end of their line to the other using only their feet. Make even teams. Each team lies on their backs in a line, head to toe. Place a hoop with objects at the feet of the first player and an empty hoop at the head of the last player. On the signal, teams pass all of their objects to the far hoop using only their feet. Play as many times as you like.

  1. Teams lie on their backs in a line from one end of the gym toward the other.
  2. Objects start at one end. Players may pass ONLY with their feet — no hands.
  3. Move every object down the line into a hoop or pile at the far end.
  4. First team to move all objects wins. If an item touches a hand or the floor off-plan, restart that item.
  5. Use it for team building; mix item sizes to change the difficulty.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–5. Teacher shows it first. No timer.
Grades 3–4. Groups of 6–8. Time trial against your own best.
Grades 5–6. Whole class or team vs team. Add a rule (no talking, fewer touches).

Safety. Agree the freeze signal and a safe way to stop. No pulling or dragging a teammate. Soft items only; no kicking an object into a face.

204. Pulse Race

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Two or more lines of 6–10 · coin or colour card · one grab-object per line

A legal pulse beats a guessed grab. Honesty is the skill.

Also called: Pulse · Daly Pulse

Equipment: Coin or colour card · one grab-object per line

Set-up: Sit or kneel in files, hands touching the next person. Object beyond the last player. First players face you.

How we play

  1. Show heads/tails or a colour only the front players can see.
  2. Tails / wrong colour: nobody moves. A squeeze here is a false start.
  3. Heads / right colour: front player squeezes. The pulse travels. Last player grabs.
  4. Fastest legal grab wins the round. Reset hands and the object.

If this happens

  • Squeeze on tails: that line cannot score the round.
  • Pulse dies mid-line: no grab, replay.
  • Last player peeks and grabs early: same as a false start.

How a round ends. First line to 5 legal grabs, or play 4–6 rounds.

Cues

  • Gentle squeeze.
  • Last player takes the ball.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Classroom sit-down.
  • Standing gym lines.
  • Hockey day: sticks parked, pulse with hands only.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Pulse; Daly Pulse.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Pulse Race for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Waits for the squeeze.
  • Character Development. Resets after a false start.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without pulling equipment away.
Grades 1–2. Teacher stands where the line can see a thumbs-up.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups design one extra rule that still includes everyone, then teach it to another group.

Safety. Ask before you pull a hoop, rope, or teammate. Stop if hair, glasses, or a wrist is caught. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

205. Reaction Speed

Cooperative & circus

When: January, March · Slot: 8-15

Hands start away from the ball. Clean drop, no fake until later.

Equipment: Trios: cone as a height marker, tennis ball

Set-up: Dropper holds the ball at chin height. Catcher ready.

How we play

  1. Dropper releases with no fake.
  2. Catcher tries to catch before the bounce.
  3. Five drops, then rotate.
Grades 1–2. Larger ball, lower drop. Allow one bounce.
Grades 3–4. Tennis ball, chin height.
Grades 5–6. Add pump-fakes after they can catch a clean drop.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. Soft tags.

206. Slippery Snakes

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class · 4 min · 2–3 long ropes.

Two students wiggle a rope on the floor while the others jump or step over it. Use it to practise jumping and timing.

Equipment: 1–2 long ropes.

Set-up: Two students wiggle a rope on the floor. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Name the shared goal out loud so the class is not chasing a winner.
  2. Others jump or step as the snake passes.
  3. Rotate wigglers every 45 seconds.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.

Cues

  • The snake stays on the floor.
  • Jump or step — do not stomp the turner’s hands.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Two snakes to cross.
  • Travel sideways.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Slippery Snakes.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Two turners wiggle a rope. The class lines up and steps or jumps over as it travels.
  • Grade 1–2 walk the snake. Grade 3+ jump a faster wiggle.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Slippery Snakes for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Jumps or steps the rope; quiet land.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Rope stays low; no whipping.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without pulling equipment away.
Grades 1–2. Walk over a still snake first.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups design one extra rule that still includes everyone, then teach it to another group.

Safety. Rope stays on the floor. Never at head height. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

207. The Great Outdoor Treasure Hunt

Cooperative & circus

When: May, June · Slot: 16–25 outdoor

An outdoor hunt where you bring the items home, one at a time — plus one secret treasure.

Equipment: Hoops as home bases, an item list

Set-up: Each student or pair places a hoop as home. Post the list of outdoor items they may collect. Hide one secret treasure.

How we play

  1. Carry only one item home at a time.
  2. Take only what is on the list; never harm plants or take what is not allowed.
  3. At the end, return every item that should go back.
  4. Count collections and see who found the secret treasure.

Video. The Great Outdoor Treasure Hunt (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-8

Equipment: Nature

An outdoor scavenger hunt where players bring the items back to a collection area, instead of just ticking them off a list. Before class, hide a secret treasure item outside (for example, a tissue box; players bring back one tissue). Each player or pair puts a hoop at the home area as their collection spot. Go over the rules and give them the item list, or post one master list at home so they must remember it. Players bring back only 1 item at a time. Choose items students can find in your area. Stress respect for property and not taking things they should not. At the end, return all items that need to go back.

  1. Each student or pair places a hoop as HOME / collection zone.
  2. Give a list of natural or outdoor items they may collect (or post one master list at home base). Hide one secret “treasure” (example: a tissue from a hidden box).
  3. Players may carry only ONE item home at a time.
  4. Respect property: take only what you listed; no harming plants or taking things that are not allowed.
  5. At the end, return every item that should be returned. Count collections and who found the secret treasure.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–5. Teacher shows it first. No timer.
Grades 3–4. Groups of 6–8. Time trial against your own best.
Grades 5–6. Whole class or team vs team. Add a rule (no talking, fewer touches).

Safety. Clear outdoor boundaries you can see. Walk in wooded or uneven areas. Stay with your partner.

Source

208. Voice Path

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Whole class or groups of 6–10 · 6–10 minutes · celebrate the group task, not a winner.

A caller uses only words to guide a partner along a path, then the partners swap. Use it to practise giving and following clear directions.

Equipment: Scattered cones. No benches.

Set-up: Pairs. Walker on one end, caller on the side. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Caller uses only words: “two steps left, stop, one step right.”
  2. Walker looks at the floor. Eyes-closed is optional and only if that student says yes.
  3. Swap roles at the far line. A bump on a cone is a pause, not a fail.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. A drop or a tangle is a restart from the last good shape, not a loss.

Cues

  • Words only.
  • Eyes-closed is a choice.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Variations

  • Eyes on the floor for most classes.
  • Smaller groups of 6 if the whole-class version tangles.
  • Silent round: only gestures, then talk again to plan.
  • Add one extra object (second balloon, extra hoop) when the first task is easy.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Voice Path.
  • Watch for one student doing all the work. Give every group a ‘must-touch’ rule.
  • Year plan: December, October — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Caller on the side. Walker to the far line. Swap.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Voice Path for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. Takes turns and solves a small problem without grabbing.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. No run; consent for eyes-closed.
  • Healthy Relationships. Gives short, kind directions.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Optional eyes-closed with consent.

Safety. No running. Caller stays on the perimeter. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

209. Wall Rally

Cooperative & circus

When: December, October · Slot: 16–25 · Wall, rope, or a square of floor markers · 1 soft ball · pairs or 2v2

Keep a legal rally going. One bounce. Point on a miss.

Also called: Cooperative Wall Ball · Hand Tennis · Floorball · Wall-ball rally · Gaga

Equipment: 1 soft ball · wall, rope, or a square of floor markers

Set-up: Small court. Underhand serve. Soft hands or a low racket.

How we play

  1. Serve so it lands on the other side or on the wall legally.
  2. Return after one bounce.
  3. More than one bounce, out, or a high racket = point. Winner serves.

If this happens

  • Fist smash: redo as an open-hand hit.
  • Ball lifted in floorball: dead ball, restart.

How a round ends. First to 7, or highest score at time.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Use names. Ask before you grab a hoop, rope, or balloon.
  • If it drops, restart together — no blame.
  • Quiet voices so the group can hear the next job.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Bench as a net.
  • Mini goals (Floorball).
  • Cone-ring gaga pit, hits below the waist.
  • Cooperative wall ball: count consecutive hits, drop restarts the count.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Cooperative Wall Ball; Hand Tennis; Floorball; Wall-ball rally; Gaga.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Cooperative Wall Ball (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details: Cooperative Wall Ball · Grades 1-8

Equipment: ball, beach ball

A teamwork game with 3 levels of difficulty. Start by asking players what co-operation means; they will find that working together gets the task done faster.

  1. Ask the class what cooperation looks like and sounds like.
  2. Level 1: Keep a playground ball going against a wall for a target number of hits. Drop = restart the count together.
  3. Level 2: Same task, but no player may touch the ball twice in a row.
  4. Level 3: Switch to a beach ball that the group must control off the wall together.
  5. Time attempts. Celebrate the group streak, not star players. Split a huge class into wall groups.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Cooperative Wall Ball for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the needed skill (pass, step-through, carry, balance) so the group can finish.
  • Skill focus. Plans a simple order or role with teammates before rushing.
  • Skill focus. The group finishes the task together.
  • Character Development. Restarts a drop without blaming a classmate.
  • Safety. Moves at a speed the group can control. Consent for partner shapes — a student may say no.
  • Healthy Relationships. Uses names and kind words; waits a turn without grabbing.
Grades 1–2. Small groups of 4–6. Teacher in the group if the task needs an extra pair of hands.
Grades 3–4. Whole-class or groups of 8. Plan for 20 seconds, then try.
Grades 5–6. Groups design one extra rule that still includes everyone, then teach it to another group.

Safety. Ask before you pull a hoop, rope, or teammate. Stop if hair, glasses, or a wrist is caught. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

Source

210. Balance Carry

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 16–25 · One object per person or pair · a finish or a court

The object stays on. If it falls, reset and continue.

Also called: Racquet waiter · Beanbag head walk · Body-part beanbags · Protect the Ice Cream · Cone Stack · Core Beanbag Rescue · Pancake Relay

Equipment: One object per person or pair (ball on cone, bag on racquet, bag on head)

Set-up: Show the carry (ball on cone, bag on racquet, bag on head). No body checks.

How we play

  1. On go, travel.
  2. If it falls, stop, replace, continue from there.
  3. Optional: knock other ice creams off for a point.

If this happens

  • Thumb pinching the ball on the whole time: remind them it must sit.
  • Shoulder charge: no point, both reset.

How a round ends. First across with it still on, or fewest drops.

Cues

  • Palm up.
  • Soft toss.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Cone on one foot, hop home (Cone Collector).
  • Bib in the air, place a cone in a hoop, catch the bib (Cone Stack).
  • Feet stay in hoop 1, rescue bags from hoop 2 (Core Beanbag Rescue).
  • Racquet waiter: bag on a racquet.
  • Beanbag head walk.
  • Body-part beanbags: named body part holds the bag.
  • Pancake Relay: small relay teams; balance a beanbag “pancake” on a tennis racket around the team cone and back, replace it if it drops, then hand racket and pancake to the next runner.

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Racquet waiter; Beanbag head walk; Body-part beanbags; Protect the Ice Cream; Cone Stack; Core Beanbag Rescue.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Racquet waiter for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Keeps the bag on the racquet to the cone.
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner or in a hoop without bumping others out.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Tosses at the hoop, not a person.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a hoop or spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Walk around a cone before the toss.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).

211. Bean Bag Core

Fitness & landings

When: February, April, January · Slot: 8-15 · Daly Exercise · slot 8-15.

From a plank, empty your hoop into someone else's. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Bean Bag Core

Equipment: One hoop and bean bags per player

Set-up: Hoops close enough to reach. Default plank is knees-down.

How we play

  1. Stay in plank. Move one bag at a time.
  2. Feet and knees do not walk the hoop.
  3. 30-45 seconds. Rest. Repeat.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Standing athletic stance.
  • Knees-down plank. 30 seconds.
  • Full plank. Non-dominant hand.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Bean Bag Core for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Bean Bag Core with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Bean Bag Core instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Bean Bag Core.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Bean Bag Core.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Standing athletic stance.
Grades 3–4. Knees-down plank. 30 seconds.
Grades 5–6. Full plank. Non-dominant hand.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

Fitness & landings

Still head, airplane arms.

Equipment: Beanbag and a hoop per team.

215. Floor rope walk

Fitness & landings

When: February · Slot: 16–25 · Lanes, spots, or a perimeter path · 6–10 minutes · quality reps, then a stretch.

Students walk a wavy floor line with airplane arms and finish with a stuck landing. Use it to practise balance without a bench.

Equipment: Rope or court lines.

Set-up: Wavy line on the floor. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Show the quality cue first (quiet land, airplane arms, still head). Count quality reps, not speed.
  2. Walk the line. Airplane arms. Step off to a stick landing.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.
  5. Play Floor rope walk in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Airplane arms.
  • Eyes on a wall spot.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Two ropes side by side for 1–2. Stork pause for 5–6.
  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.
  • Feet-only hockey-ball version if sticks are new or the class is tired.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Floor rope walk.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Sticks: teach grip and ‘blade down’ in the hallway line before the first dribble.
  • Year plan: February — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Walk the rope or court line. Step off to a stick.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Floor rope walk for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Walks the line and sticks the step-off.
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner without bumping others.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Waits for the line to be free.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain. Feet-only if sticks are new.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Show three quality reps. Add a half-turn, opposite hand, or a hoop target on the last set.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. Sticks stay below the knee. No slapshots. Blade on the floor when you stop. Floor ropes or court lines only — no benches. One walker on a line. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

216. Frogs on the Lily Pads

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 5–16 or 16–25 · Whole class · 5 min · hoop path.

Students jump with two feet from hoop to hoop and stick each landing. Use it to practise gymnastics landings.

How we play

  1. Show the quality cue first (quiet land, airplane arms, still head). Count quality reps, not speed.

  3. A miss means start that path again.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.

Cues

  • Bend–jump–stick.
  • Miss = start that path again.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

  • Two-to-one landings.
  • Carry a beanbag on the head.
  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Frogs on the Lily Pads.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Sticks: teach grip and ‘blade down’ in the hallway line before the first dribble.
  • Year plan: February, April — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Hoop lily pads in 3–4 paths. Jump hoop to hoop and hold the land for a count of 3.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Frogs on the Lily Pads for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Jump-stick or a still shape for 3 seconds.
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner or in a hoop without bumping others out.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. One at a time on a hoop or mat.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a hoop or spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain. Feet-only if sticks are new.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Farther pads. Quiet landings.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. Sticks stay below the knee. No slapshots. Blade on the floor when you stop. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

218. Hoop Balance

  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Hoop Balance.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss or a tag in Hoop Balance.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle. Soft tags.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate re-enter after a miss or a tag.
Grades 1–2. Allow one fingertip.
Grades 3–4. Shorter distance.
Grades 5–6. Three people, one hoop.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. Soft two-finger or noodle tags on the back or shoulder — never the head. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

219. Immersive Workout ADVENTURE All Ages Brain Break

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · 1-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

Project this 5-minute adventure video, and students copy the jogging, jumping, swinging and climbing actions. Use it as a warm-up or brain break.

Equipment: Projector

Set-up: Project the official 5-minute adventure video so the whole class can see the screen. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Students stand in personal space facing the screen.
  2. Play the video. Students copy every travel and fitness action (jog, jump, swing, climb, etc.).
  3. Teacher models energy and safe spacing. Pause only if a movement needs a quick reteach.
  4. Use as a warm-up, brain break, or indoor instant activity.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  6. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Immersive Workout ADVENTURE All Ages Brain Break.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Year plan: February, April — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Immersive Workout ADVENTURE All Ages Brain Break (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 1-8

Equipment: Projector

A 5-minute active warm-up video with an adventure theme set around the world. It includes jogging, jumping, swinging, climbing and more. A PHYSEDGAMES video for PE class.

  1. Project the official 5-minute adventure video so the whole class can see the screen.
  2. Students stand in personal space facing the screen.
  3. Play the video. Students copy every travel and fitness action (jog, jump, swing, climb, etc.).
  4. Teacher models energy and safe spacing. Pause only if a movement needs a quick reteach.
  5. Use as a warm-up, brain break, or indoor instant activity.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in the bout for the set time; names breath or warmth after.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the called movement (run, skip, jump, freeze).
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner without bumping others.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landings; freeze on the signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Show three quality reps. Add a half-turn, opposite hand, or a hoop target on the last set.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

220. Inchworm Rescue

Fitness & landings

When: April · Slot: 16-25 · Daly Exercise · slot 16-25.

Feet stay planted. Walk the hands out and back with one ball. Daly Exercise game. Nobody sits the period.

Also called: Daly Exercise: Inchworm Rescue

Equipment: Two hoops per pupil; five balls in the far hoop

Set-up: Feet in home hoop. Five balls wait in hoop 2.

How we play

  1. Walk hands out, grab one ball, walk back, drop it in hoop 1.
  2. One ball per trip.
  3. Repeat until all five are home.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Fewer balls. A knee down is legal.
  • Five balls. Feet stay planted.
  • Time cap.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Inchworm Rescue for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Inchworm Rescue with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Inchworm Rescue instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Inchworm Rescue.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss in Inchworm Rescue.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle. Soft tags. No throws at a face.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss.
Grades 1–2. Fewer balls. A knee down is legal.
Grades 3–4. Five balls. Feet stay planted.
Grades 5–6. Time cap.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. Soft two-finger or noodle tags on the back or shoulder — never the head. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

221. Jump The River

Fitness & landings

When: April, May · Slot: 0–5 or 16–25

Two ropes are the river banks. Jump across, and the river gets wider after each full-class success.

Equipment: 2 skipping ropes (or court lines)

Set-up: Lay two ropes parallel as river banks, starting close together.

How we play

  1. Jump the river: two-foot take-off and landing, or a running long jump for older grades.
  2. After the whole class gets across, widen the ropes a little.
  3. Touching a rope or the “water” means a quick swim motion or 5 bank jumps, then try again — nobody is out.
  4. Vary the approach: hop, jump-turn, partner jump.

Video. Jump The River (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-8

Equipment: 2 skipping ropes

A simple jumping activity, useful for long jump practice. Idea credit: Deric Hafer.

  1. Lay two jump ropes parallel as river banks, starting close together.
  2. Students jump the river (two-foot takeoff and landing, or a running long jump for older grades).
  3. After a successful full-class pass, widen the ropes a little.
  4. Touching a rope or the “water” means a quick swim motion or 5 bank jumps, then try again — do not eliminate anyone.
  5. Vary the approach: hop, jump-turn, partner jump.

Source · Video

Grades 1–2. Short distance, two-foot take-off, focus on a quiet landing.
Grades 3–4. Widen the challenge a little after the whole class succeeds.
Grades 5–6. Add a run-up or a one-foot take-off. Measure against your own best.

Safety. Land on two feet with bent knees. One jumper per lane; wait until the landing area is clear.

Source

222. Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge

Fitness & landings

When: February, May · Slot: 8-15 · Daly Exercise · slot 8-15.

Students spin a hoop, do jumping jacks, then catch it, adding one jack after each clean catch. Use it as a personal challenge, not a race (Daly Exercise game).

Also called: Daly Exercise: Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge

Equipment: One hoop each; personal space

Set-up: Show a clean rim-spin and a two-hand catch.

How we play

  1. Spin the hoop upright on the floor.
  2. Do 1 jumping jack, then catch.
  3. Next try: 2, then 3. A drop returns you to your last clean number.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset kit, then the next round.

If this happens

  • Kit knocks a person: pause, widen space, restart.
  • Argument over a point: teacher call, play on.
  • Someone sits out after a miss: they rejoin on the next turn with a short task instead.

How a round ends. Play 3–6 minutes, or first team/player to the posted target, then rotate jobs.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • One job at a time. Extra players fetch or count.
  • A miss is a short task, then back in.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Marching instead of jacks.
  • Jacks. Quality over count.
  • Record a weekly personal best.

Teaching tips

  • Source: Daly Exercise. Same house rules as the rest of the playbook.
  • Watch for crowding at one piece of kit. Add a second station before you add speed.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Uses the taught action in Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge with control, not a rush that knocks kit or people.
  • Skill focus. Chooses space, timing, or a partner job in Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge instead of crowding.
  • Skill focus. Takes a role and shares kit in Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge.
  • Character Development. Honest about a miss or a drop in Jumping Jack Hoop Challenge.
  • Safety. Freeze on the whistle.
  • Healthy Relationships. Helps a classmate try again after a miss or a drop.
Grades 1–2. Marching instead of jacks.
Grades 3–4. Jacks. Quality over count.
Grades 5–6. Record a weekly personal best.

Safety. No elimination. Freeze on the whistle. A miss is a short task, then stay in.

223. Knee-ball jumps

Fitness & landings

When: May, June · Slot: 16–25 · Soft ball, short lane.

Students jump to a cone and back holding a ball between their knees. Use it to practise controlled jumping.

Equipment: Soft ball, two cones.

Set-up: Short lane. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Show the quality cue first (quiet land, airplane arms, still head). Count quality reps, not speed.
  2. Ball between the knees. Jump to the cone and back. Reset if it drops.
  3. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  4. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.
  5. Play Knee-ball jumps in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Squeeze.
  • Quiet land.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Walk-squeeze for 1–2.
  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Knee-ball jumps.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Year plan: May, June — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Ball between the knees. Jump the lane. Reset if it drops.

Alberta PEW outcomes

  • Active Living. Stays in Knee-ball jumps for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Jumps and sticks with the ball held.
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner without bumping others.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landing.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Add a hoop to drop the ball into.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

224. Long Jump Challenge

Fitness & landings

When: February, April, May · Slot: 8-15

Beat the team mark from the same line.

Equipment: 1 cone per team; jump line

Set-up: Clear landing area. Shared jump line.

225. Magic Numbers

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 0–5 or fitness · Whole class · 4 min.

Students do 10 (or 7) of each of three movements without stopping. Use it for a short fitness sequence.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Name three movements that fit the month (travel, jump, shape). Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Show the quality cue first (quiet land, airplane arms, still head). Count quality reps, not speed.
  2. Do 10 (or 7) of each without stopping.
  3. In April, use tuck / star / stretch language.
  4. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  5. Play Magic Numbers in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Make the number with bodies.
  • Still for 3 seconds.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Must use a level change.
  • Add a locomotor to collect the group.
  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Magic Numbers.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Year plan: February, April — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Call a number. Groups build it lying or standing (a 4, an 8).
  • Or call a sum (‘make 10’) and they choose a group size.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Magic Numbers for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Finds a group or a spot without diving.
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Leftovers stay in — they join the teacher or the next round.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landings, space to jump, freeze on the signal. No comparing scores aloud.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. 10-10-10 plus a direction change.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

226. Mass Challenges

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 0–5 · Whole class · 6–8 min.

Name a three-part challenge, and the whole class performs it together. Use it to practise moving in unison.

Equipment: None.

Set-up: Scatter with space. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Teacher names a 3-part challenge (3 slides left, jump high, freeze in a shape).
  2. Class performs in unison. End with a clap.
  3. Repeat with a student-built challenge.
  4. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.
  5. Play Mass Challenges in short rounds so more students get the key job, then freeze and reset.

Cues

  • Whole class succeeds or you reset the challenge — no one is cut.
  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Must use only two body parts.
  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Mass Challenges.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Year plan: February, April — match the month’s sport ball when you can.
  • Give a class problem: move all the beanbags in 30 seconds; everyone across the gym touching only mats; keep three beach balls up for 20 seconds.
  • If you fail, change one rule and try again.

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in Mass Challenges for the set time; can name breath or enjoyment after — not a ranking.
  • Movement Skill Development. Finds a group or a spot without diving.
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Leftovers stay in — they join the teacher or the next round.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landings, space to jump, freeze on the signal. No comparing scores aloud.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a landing.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Show three quality reps. Add a half-turn, opposite hand, or a hoop target on the last set.

Safety. Still ending. No tumbling unless on mats in April. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one.

227. Minute Fitness

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · 3-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

Students work for 1 minute at a strong effort, then rest for 20 seconds, for 8–12 rounds. Use it as an interval fitness activity.

Equipment: None (or a variety)

Set-up: Students spread out in personal space. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Optional: put up exercise posters so they can choose movements. Free posters: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1clLqzemKN-USZ7tOMx2oXah6YKJVVBC3/view
  2. Work 1 minute at a strong effort (jumping jacks, squats, high knees, plank, mountain climbers, etc.).
  3. Rest 20 seconds (walk / shake out).
  4. Repeat 8–12 rounds. Students may change exercises each work interval.
  5. Cool down with easy walking and name one exercise that raised heart rate.
  6. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  7. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Minute Fitness.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Year plan: February, April — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Minute Fitness (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

Equipment: None (or a variety)

229. Pair Running

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 16–25 · Large group · 3-8 · 5–8 min rounds.

Partners take turns running a loop while the other recovers, so one partner is always running. Use it for pacing practice (Paarlauf, a German relay method; idea from Brian Loeppky).

Equipment: None

Set-up: This is Paarlauf (“pair run”), a German relay method. Agree the freeze signal before the first round.

How we play

  1. Partner A starts running a loop or shuttle. Partner B waits at the exchange point.
  2. A tags / hands off to B. B runs while A recovers. They keep exchanging so one partner is always running.
  3. Use a track, gym perimeter, or out-and-back cones. Set a time (3–6 minutes) rather than a distance so every pair stays moving.
  4. Coach even pacing and a gentle tag handoff — no shoving.
  5. Freeze on the whistle. Reset spots, then the next round.
  6. After two rounds, 20 seconds of a named stretch, then one more quality set.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Variations

  • Half distance or fewer reps for grades 1–2. Quality landings stay the same.
  • Partner count: one moves, one counts out loud, then swap.
  • Last round is ‘show your best three’ — not a race.

Teaching tips

  • Reset: freeze, walk back to start spots, change the key role, then GO. Two or three short rounds beat one long messy one in Pair Running.
  • Watch for sloppy landings when they race. Sit the race, keep the quality set.
  • Year plan: February, April — match the month’s sport ball when you can.

Video. Pair Running (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades 3-8

Equipment: None

A German running method; the name means “pair run.” Idea credit: Brian Loeppky.

  1. This is Paarlauf (“pair run”), a German relay method.
  2. Partner A starts running a loop or shuttle. Partner B waits at the exchange point.
  3. A tags / hands off to B. B runs while A recovers. They keep exchanging so one partner is always running.
  4. Use a track, gym perimeter, or out-and-back cones. Set a time (3–6 minutes) rather than a distance so every pair stays moving.
  5. Coach even pacing and a gentle tag handoff — no shoving.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

  • Active Living. Stays in the bout for the set time; names breath or warmth after.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the called movement (run, skip, jump, freeze).
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner without bumping others.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landings; freeze on the signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a turn.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Show three quality reps. Add a half-turn, opposite hand, or a hoop target on the last set.

231. Rainbow Road

Fitness & landings

When: February, April · Slot: 16–25 · Scattered colours · home base

Go to the colour that was called. One object or one touch per call.

Also called: Memory Relay · Treasure Hunt · Card Sharks · Colour Hunt · Memory Colour

Equipment: Scattered colour objects · home base

Set-up: Scatter colours. Class starts in a home base. You will name a colour and a travel skill.

How we play

  1. Call ‘skip to red.’ Collect or touch that colour, return.
  2. Next call: new colour, new travel.
  3. Progression: a sequence of three they must remember.

If this happens

  • Two items taken: extra goes back.
  • Wrong colour: return it, no score.

How a round ends. Most correct collections, or first pair back from a sequence.

Cues

  • Freeze on the whistle — then eyes on the teacher.
  • Quiet landings. Bend ankles, knees, and hips.
  • Quality beats speed. Stop if a wrist, knee, or back complains.
  • Breathe out on the work; do not hold a breath to rush.

Skins — same game, different wrapper

  • Cups over hidden colours (Memory Colour).
  • Memory Relay: sequence of three colours.
  • Card Sharks: flip a card for the next colour.
  • Outdoor controls with a simple map (keep Partner Orienteering separate if you want real map work).

Teaching tips

  • Also called: Memory Relay; The Great Outdoor Treasure Hunt; Treasure Hunt; Card Sharks; Colour Hunt; Memory Colour.
  • House rules: Freeze on the whistle. Tags are two-finger or noodle on the back or shoulder. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher). No elimination.

Video. Rainbow Road (PhysEdGames)

Clips are demos. Our house rules still apply (no elimination, soft tag; dodgeballs stay on the Dodgeball page).

More details · Grades K-4

Equipment: Coloured spots (lilly pads), or hoops

Teams move along floor spots in the colour pattern you call. Make 4 teams, each against a side wall. Place coloured spots or hoops around the gym. Call a pattern, for example “red, blue, green, blue, yellow.” Players run from spot to spot in that order, then return to their team’s side. Call a new pattern each round, and change the movement skill (hopping, skipping, galloping, lunging).

  1. Create 4 teams, each against a different side wall.
  2. Scatter coloured spots or hoops all over the gym.
  3. Call a colour pattern (“red, blue, green, blue, yellow”). All players run spot-to-spot in that order, then return to their wall.
  4. Call a new pattern. Change the locomotor skill (hop, skip, gallop, lunge) each round.

Source · Video

Alberta PEW outcomes

Pick one or two look-fors per class.

  • Active Living. Stays in the bout for the set time; names breath or warmth after.
  • Movement Skill Development. Matches the called movement (run, skip, jump, freeze).
  • Skill focus. Paces the set so they can finish, not sprint and collapse.
  • Skill focus. Works beside a partner without bumping others.
  • Character Development. Records a private number honestly, or skips the number and names effort.
  • Safety. Soft landings; freeze on the signal.
  • Healthy Relationships. Lets a classmate have a spot; no blocking a turn.
Grades 1–2. Half the distance or fewer reps. Knees-down options. Stop if wrists or backs complain.
Grades 3–4. Full lane or full set. Quiet landings. Partner counts.
Grades 5–6. Show three quality reps. Add a half-turn, opposite hand, or a hoop target on the last set.

Safety. Quiet landings. Stop a station if a wrist, knee, or back complains — switch to a walk option. No elimination: a sit is a stretch, a jail, a hospital, or a role swap, then back in. Freeze on the whistle. Watch a demo or clip before the first play if you have one. A miss is a short task and you stay in. Extra players get a job (bumper, next wave, fetcher).
